Tree decompositions meet induced matchings: beyond Max Weight Independent Set Thanks: Lima is supported by the Independent Research Fund Denmark grant agreement number 2098-00012B. Milanič, Muršič, and Štorgel are supported in part by the Slovenian Research and Innovation Agency (Milanič: I0-0035, research program P1-0285 and research projects J1-3001, J1-3002, J1-3003, J1-4008, J1-4084, and N1-0102; Muršič: research project J1-4008; Štorgel: research program P1-0383 and research projects J1-3002 and J1-4008). Milanič is also supported in part by the research program CogniCom (0013103) at the University of Primorska. Okrasa is supported by the Polish National Science Centre grant no. 2021/41/N/ST6/01507. Rzążewski is supported by the European Research Council (ERC) under the European Union’s Horizon 2020 research and innovation programme grant agreement number 948057.
Paloma T. Lima, Martin Milanič, Peter Muršič, Karolina Okrasa, Paweł Rzążewski, Kenny Štorgel

Abstract
For a tree decomposition 𝒯 \mathcal{T} of a graph G G , by μ ⁡ ( 𝒯 ) \mu(\mathcal{T}) we denote the size of a largest induced matching in G G all of whose edges intersect one bag of 𝒯 \mathcal{T} . Induced matching treewidth of a graph G G is the minimum value of μ ⁡ ( 𝒯 ) \mu(\mathcal{T}) over all tree decompositions 𝒯 \mathcal{T} of G G . Yolov [SODA 2018] proved that Max Weight Independent Set can be solved in polynomial time for graphs of bounded induced matching treewidth. In this paper we explore what other problems are tractable in such classes of graphs. As our main result, we give a polynomial-time algorithm for Min Weight Feedback Vertex Set . We also provide some positive results concerning packing induced subgraphs, which in particular imply a PTAS for the problem of finding a largest induced subgraph of bounded treewidth. These results suggest that in graphs of bounded induced matching treewidth, one could find in polynomial time a maximum-weight induced subgraph of bounded treewidth satisfying a given CMSO 2 formula. We conjecture that such a result indeed holds and prove it for graphs of bounded tree-independence number , which form a rich and important family of subclasses of graphs of bounded induced matching treewidth. We complement these algorithmic results with a number of complexity and structural results concerning induced matching treewidth.



1
Introduction

Structured graph decompositions and the corresponding graph width parameters have become one of the central tools for dealing with algorithmically hard graph problems.
One of the best known and well-studied graph width parameters is
treewidth
, which was introduced independently by several groups of authors with various motivations
[
16
,
41
,
64
,
8
]
.
Roughly speaking, treewidth measures how similar the graph is to a tree.
A graph
G
with small treewidth can be represented by a tree
T
whose every node holds a small subset (called
bag
) of vertices of
V(G)
, such that the connectivity properties of
G
are reflected in the local structure of
T
(and the bags).
A tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}
consists of the tree
T
and a function that assigns a bag to each node of
T
,
and the
width
of
\mathcal{T}
is the size of its largest bag minus 1 (this is by convention).
The
treewidth

\mathrm{tw}(G)
is the minimum width of a tree decomposition of
G
.

Having a tree decomposition of small width is excellent for algorithmic applications, as we can mimic the standard bottom-up dynamic programming on trees.
For example, in order to solve
Max Weight Independent Set
(
MWIS
), the dynamic programming table for each node of
T
should be indexed by all possible independent sets in the corresponding bag.
Consequently, if each bag is
small
, say, its size is bounded by a constant
k
, the running time we obtain is
2^{k}\cdot(n+|V(T)|)^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
where
n\coloneqq|V(G)|
.
As
|V(T)|
can also be assumed to be polynomial in
n
, this yields a polynomial-time algorithm for
MWIS
when restricted to graphs of bounded treewidth.

Such an approach has found numerous applications in solving classic
NP
-hard problems on graphs with bounded treewidth; see the handbook by Cygan et al.
[
28
, Section 7]
.
The richness of the family of problems that can be solved by a dynamic programming over a tree decomposition is witnessed by the celebrated meta-theorem by Courcelle
[
27
]
. It asserts that each problem expressible in
Counting Monadic Second Order Logic
(
CMSO
2
)
1
1

1

In this logic one can use vertex, edge, and (vertex or edge) set variables,
check vertex-edge incidence, quantify over variables,
and apply counting predicates modulo fixed integers. See
Section

6
for a formal introduction.
can be solved in polynomial time (actually, in linear time) for graphs of bounded treewidth.

An astute reader might notice a caveat in the results mentioned above: they assume that the input graph is given with a corresponding decomposition. This might be a serious obstacle, as computing an optimal tree decomposition is
NP
-hard
[
8
,
18
]
.
However, if treewidth is bounded by a constant, an optimal decomposition can be found in polynomial time
[
17
,
47
]
.
Actually, in the context discussed above even an approximation of an optimal decomposition is sufficient.
Luckily, such approximation algorithms are known not only for treewidth
[
65
,
19
,
45
]
, but also for other parameters discussed in the paper
[
71
,
29
]
.
Thus, we will implicitly assume that the instance graphs are always provided with a corresponding tree decomposition.

Let us go back to the
MWIS
problem, which serves as the starting point of our investigations.
Notice that in the argument sketched above we do not really need the fact that the treewidth is bounded by a constant.
Indeed, if the size of each bag is bounded by a
logarithmic function of
n
, then the number of all independent sets in a single bag is bounded by
2^{\mathcal{O}(\log n)}=n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
, i.e., by a polynomial, which still yields a polynomial-time algorithm for
MWIS
. This is one of the motivations to study classes of graphs with
logarithmic treewidth
,
which has been a very active topic in structural graph theory in recent years
[
2
,
21
]
.

However, the crux of the algorithm above is not really the size of the bag, but the number of independent sets inside each bag.
Consider, for example, the class of chordal graphs, i.e., graphs that do not contain an induced cycle with at least four vertices. Equivalently, these are graphs that admit a tree decomposition whose every bag is a clique.
Even though these cliques can be arbitrarily large, they still contain very few (that is, polynomially many) independent sets and thus such a decomposition can be used to solve
MWIS
in polynomial time.

This observation is heavily extended by the framework of
potential maximal cliques
by Bouchitté and Todinca
[
23
]
. Essentially, there we work with an implicitly given tree decomposition whose every bag is promised to contain only a constant number of vertices from the optimal independent set.
This approach allows us, for example, to solve
MWIS
and many related problems for graph classes with polynomially many minimal separators
[
23
,
36
]
.
With some care, it can also be used for other graph classes, e.g., graphs that exclude a fixed induced path
[
51
,
40
]
or long induced cycles
[
3
]
.

Another, more direct way of generalizing bounded-treewidth graphs and chordal graphs was suggested by Yolov
[
71
]
and later, independently, by Dallard, Milanič, and Štorgel
[
32
]
. We use the notation and terminology of Dallard et al.
[
32
]
.
Given a tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}
of a graph
G
, by
\alpha(\mathcal{T})
we denote the size of a largest independent set contained in a bag of
\mathcal{T}
. The
tree-independence number
of
G
, denoted by
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G)
, is the minimum value of
\alpha(\mathcal{T})
over all tree decompositions
\mathcal{T}
of
G
.
Note that given a tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}
of
G
with
\alpha(\mathcal{T})\leqslant k
, we can solve
MWIS
on
G
in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
, which is polynomial for constant
k
.
Let us remark that we always have
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G)\leqslant\mathrm{tw}(G)+1
and if
G
is chordal, then
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G)\leqslant 1
(actually, the reverse implication also holds
[
32
]
).
Tree-independence number proved to be a fruitful topic in recent years and attracted some attention, both with structural
[
30
,
1
]
and algorithmic
[
32
,
29
]
motivations.

In a recent manuscript, Dallard, Fomin, Golovach, Korhonen, and Milanič
[
29
]
showed that tree-independence number is in a sense the most general “natural” parameter of a tree decomposition whose boundedness yields a polynomial-time algorithm for
MWIS
.
More precisely, let
\gamma
be any graph invariant satisfying
\gamma(G-v)\leqslant\gamma(G)\leqslant\gamma(G-v)+1
for any graph
G
and any
v\in V(G)
.
For a tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}
of a graph
G
, define
\gamma(\mathcal{T})
as the maximum value of
\gamma(B)
taken over all subgraphs
B
induced by a single bag of
\mathcal{T}
.
By
\gamma_{\textrm{tw}}(G)
we denote the minimum value of
\gamma(\mathcal{T})
over all tree decompositions
\mathcal{T}
of
G
.
2
2

2

While not immediately relevant for our paper, it may be worth mentioning that such tree decomposition based parameters can also be defined and studied in the more general context of hypergraphs (see
[
5
,
53
,
71
]
).

Note that if
\gamma(G)=|V(G)|-1
, then
\gamma_{\textrm{tw}}
is precisely treewidth, and if
\gamma(G)
is the size of a largest independent set in
G
, then
\gamma_{\textrm{tw}}
is the tree-independence number.
Dallard et al.
[
29
]
showed that for every invariant
\gamma
as above, either
\gamma_{\textrm{tw}}(G)
is upper-bounded by a function of
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G)
(i.e., boundedness of
\gamma_{\textrm{tw}}(G)
implies boundedness of
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G)
), or
MWIS
remains
NP
-hard even for graphs
G
with constant
\gamma_{\textrm{tw}}(G)
.

Four years earlier, Yolov
[
71
]
(obviously unaware of the work of Dallard et al.
[
29
]
) had defined another, “stronger” parameter of a tree decomposition that can still be used to solve
MWIS
. Of course there is no contradiction here – Yolov’s parameter does not fall into the category of “natural” parameters considered by Dallard et al.
In the original paper of Yolov
[
71
]
, the parameter in question is called
minor-matching hypertree width
and is defined in a much more general setting of hypergraphs.
As such a general definition is not relevant to our work, let us focus on the case of graphs; here we will call this parameter
induced matching treewidth
.
For a tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}
of
G
, by
\mu(\mathcal{T})
let us denote the size of a largest induced matching in
G
all of whose edges intersect a single bag of
\mathcal{T}
.
Now, the induced matching treewidth of
G
, denoted by
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G)
, is the minimum value of
\mu(\mathcal{T})
over all tree decompositions
\mathcal{T}
of
G
.
Note that
\mu(\mathcal{T})
does not depend only on subgraphs induced by single bags, as some edges of an induced matching defining
\mu(\mathcal{T})
might have one endpoint outside the bag.

It follows immediately from the definitions that for every graph it holds that
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G)\leqslant\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G)
.
On the other hand, boundedness of induced matching treewidth does not imply boundedness of tree-independence number: for a biclique
K_{n,n}
with each part of size
n
we have
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(K_{n,n})=1
but, as observed by Dallard et al.
[
32
]
,
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(K_{n,n})=n
.
Thus indeed, induced matching treewidth is a “stronger” parameter.

It is also important to note that the families of graph classes with bounded induced matching treewidth and classes of graphs with polynomially many minimal separators are incomparable.
For example, the class of all graphs
G_{k}
consisting of
k
internally disjoint paths of length three with the same endpoints has bounded treewidth and hence bounded induced matching treewidth, but graphs in the class have exponentially many minimal separators (it is not difficult to see that the graph
G_{k}
has at least
2^{k}
minimal separators).
On other other hand,
P_{4}
-free graphs have a polynomial number of minimal separators (see
[
57
]
) but unbounded induced matching treewidth.
This follows from a construction that we will present later in the paper, in
Proposition

7.10
.

At first it is not clear why boundedness of induced matching treewidth is helpful in solving
MWIS
.
However, the following structural result by Yolov
[
71
]
is the key to understanding the connection between these two notions.
Let
\mathcal{T}
be a tree decomposition of
G
with
\mu(\mathcal{T})\leqslant k
.
Then, for any node
t
of
\mathcal{T}
, in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
we can enumerate a family
\mathbb{I}_{t}
such that
for any inclusion-wise maximal independent set
I
of
G
, the intersection of
I
with the bag associated with
t
is in
\mathbb{I}_{t}
.
Since every
maximum
independent set is in particular
maximal
, this immediately implies that the number of ways an optimal solution might intersect each bag of
\mathcal{T}
is polynomial in
n
, if
k
is a constant.
Using also the fact that given a graph with induced matching treewidth at most
k
, in polynomial time we can compute a tree decomposition such that
\mu(\mathcal{T})=\mathcal{O}(k)
(see
Theorem

3.3
in
Section

3
), we obtain the following algorithmic result as a consequence.

Theorem 1.1
.

For every fixed
k
, the
MWIS
problem on
n
-vertex graphs
G
with
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G)\leqslant k
can be solved in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
.

The tools developed by Yolov
[
71
]
can also be used to solve other problems that boil down to finding a constant number of (maximal) independent sets, like
r
-
Coloring
or finding a homomorphism to a fixed target graph. Even though this was not stated explicitly in
[
71
]
, one can also obtain a polynomial-time algorithm for the problem of finding a largest induced
r
-colorable subgraph. For
r=2
this problem is equivalent (by taking the complement of the solution) to
Min Odd Cycle Transversal
.

The main goal of this paper is to explore what other problems that do not fall into the above category can be solved in polynomial time in classes of bounded induced matching treewidth.
Recall that for bounded-treewidth graphs, a rich family of tractable problems is provided by the meta-theorem of Courcelle
[
27
]
.
For graphs
G
with polynomially many minimal separators, where the framework of potential maximal cliques can be applied, a somewhat similar general result is provided by Fomin, Todinca, and Villanger
[
36
]
. For fixed integer
r
and fixed
\mathsf{CMSO}_{2}
formula
\psi
,
by
(r,\psi)
-
MWIS
we denote the following computational problem (here “
MWIS
” stands for
Max Weight Induced Subgraph
).

Input:

A graph
G
equipped with a weight function
\mathfrak{w}\colon V(G)\to\mathbb{Q}_{+}
.

Task:

Find a set
F\subseteq V(G)
, such that

•

G[F]\models\psi
,

•

\mathrm{tw}(G[F])\leqslant r
,

•

F
is of maximum weight subject to the conditions above,

or conclude that no such set exists.

(r,\psi)
-
MWIS

Fomin, Todinca, and Villanger
[
36
]
proved that for each fixed
r
and
\psi
, the
(r,\psi)
-
MWIS
problem can be solved in time polynomial in the size of the input graph
G
and the number of minimal separators in
G
.
Thus the running time is polynomial when restricted to classes of graphs with polynomial number of minimal separators.

Algorithms for
(r,\psi)
-
MWIS
are also known for graphs excluding a fixed induced path, or graphs excluding long induced cycles
[
3
,
37
]
.
We conjecture that
(r,\psi)
-
MWIS
can also be solved in polynomial time for graphs of bounded induced matching treewidth.

Conjecture 1.2
.

For every fixed
k,r
and a
\mathsf{CMSO}_{2}
formula
\psi
, the
(r,\psi)
-
MWIS
problem can be solved in polynomial time for graphs with induced matching treewidth at most
k
.

Even though we are not (yet) able to prove
1.2
, we provide some substantial evidence by approaching it from three different directions.

2
Overview of our results

The paper contains three main algorithmic contributions, as well as an initial set of results regarding induced matching treewidth, including computational results and bounds.
We give an overview of these four sets of results and their implications in the following subsections.

2.1
Solving
Max Weight Induced Forest
.

As our first and main result, we show that in graphs of bounded induced matching treewidth, one can in polynomial time find an induced forest of maximum possible weight.

Theorem 2.1
.

For every fixed
k
, the
Max Weight Induced Forest
problem on
n
-vertex graphs
G
with
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G)\leqslant k
can be solved in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
.

Note that
Max Weight Induced Forest
is equivalent to finding a maximum-weight induced subgraph of treewidth at most 1, i.e., is a special case of
(r,\psi)
-
MWIS
for
r=1
and
\psi
being any formula satisfied by all graphs.
Furthermore, by complementation,
Max Weight Induced Forest
is equivalent to the well-studied
Min Weight Feedback Vertex Set
problem.

Before sketching the proof of
Theorem

2.1
, let us recall the textbook algorithm for
Max Weight Induced Forest
for graphs of bounded treewidth (see
[
28
]
).
Let
\mathcal{T}
be a tree decomposition of the instance graph,
t
be a node of
\mathcal{T}
, and
X_{t}
be the bag corresponding to
t
.
For each set
Z\subseteq X_{t}
and each partition
\pi
of
Z
,
we keep the maximum weight of an induced forest
F
contained in the subgraph of
G
induced by the bags of the subtree of
\mathcal{T}
rooted at
t
, such that:

•

the intersection of
V(F)
with
X_{t}
is exactly
Z
, and

•

the partition
\pi
corresponds to the connected components of
F
.

For any induced forest
F
in
G
, we call a pair
(Z,\pi)
as above the
signature of
F
at
t
.
Now, processing
\mathcal{T}
in a bottom-up fashion, we can find a maximum-weight induced forest in
G
using the information stored for each node.

The key insight leading to the proof of
Theorem

2.1
is the following structural lemma.

Lemma 2.2

(Simplified statement of
Lemma

4.1
)
.

Let
k
be a fixed integer. For an
n
-vertex graph
G
, a tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}
of
G
with
\mu(\mathcal{T})\leqslant k
, and a node
t
of
\mathcal{T}
,
in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
we can enumerate a set
\mathbb{F}_{t}
that contains the signature at
t
of every maximal induced forest in
G
.

Recall that an analogous result for
MWIS
was shown by Yolov
[
71
]
: for each bag
X_{t}
corresponding to a node
t
of a tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}
of bounded
\mu(\mathcal{T})
, in polynomial time we can enumerate a family
\mathbb{I}_{t}
that contains an intersection of each maximal independent set in
G
with
X_{t}
.

As every maximum induced forest is in particular inclusion-wise maximal,
Lemma

2.2
allows us to solve
Max Weight Induced Forest
by essentially mimicking the textbook algorithm for bounded-treewidth graphs.
We just need to find the set
\mathbb{F}_{t}
for each node
t
of
\mathcal{T}
, and instead of indexing the dynamic programming table by
all
possible pairs
(Z,\pi)
, we use just the pairs that are in
\mathbb{F}_{t}
.

So let us describe the proof of
Lemma

2.2
.
Let
F
be a maximal induced forest in
G
; we think of it as an (unknown) optimal solution. Consider a node
t
of
\mathcal{T}
and its corresponding bag
X_{t}
.
The difficulty that we need to face is that the intersection of
F
with
X_{t}
might be arbitrarily large; for example, a component of
F
might be a large induced star contained in
X_{t}
. Thus we need to find some compact way to encode such possible intersections.

Let

be the
skeleton of
F
, i.e., the set of vertices of degree at least two in
F
together with an arbitrary but fixed vertex
v_{C}
of each two-vertex component
C
of
F
.
By
L(F)
and
T(F)
we denote, respectively, the sets of vertices of degree 1 (
leaves
) and of degree 0 (
trivial
vertices) of
F
, with the exception that for each two-vertex component
C
of
F
we include in
L(F)
exactly one vertex from
C
, namely the vertex of
C
different from
v_{C}
.

is of size bounded by a function of

Indeed, if

was too large, we could easily extract from

an induced matching whose every edge intersects the bag.

.

So we are left with finding an encoding of the set
(L(F)\cup T(F))\cap X_{t}
.
Observe that
\dddot{\text{\textleaf}}({F})\coloneqq L(F)\cup T(F)
is an independent set in
G
.
However, this is not very useful, as
L(F)\cup T(F)
does not have to be maximal and we only have some information about the intersections of maximal independent sets with
X_{t}
(they have to be in
\mathbb{I}_{t}
, which is of polynomial size).
Let
I^{*}(F)
be some maximal independent set of
G
containing
\dddot{\text{\textleaf}}({F})
; we know that its intersection
I
with
X_{t}
can only be chosen in a polynomial number of ways.
However,
I
contains some vertices that are not in the solution (called
impostors
) and we should not consider their weight when choosing an optimal solution.
Why does a vertex
v\in I
not belong to
\dddot{\text{\textleaf}}({F})
?
As
F
is a maximal forest, there are two possible reasons why
v
is an impostor:

N[X_{t}]
, where by
N[X_{t}]
we denote the set consisting of the vertices from
X_{t}
and their neighbors.
If
v
has no neighbors in
\widehat{S}
, then
v
is trivial, i.e.,
v\in T(F)
.
If
v
has one neighbor in
\widehat{S}
, then
v
is a leaf, i.e.,
v\in L(F)
.
Finally, if
v
has at least two neighbors in
\widehat{S}
, then
v
is an impostor, i.e.,
v\in I\setminus\dddot{\text{\textleaf}}({F})
.
If we could store the information about the set
\widehat{S}
explicitly, we could easily distinguish between these three types of vertices.
Unfortunately, the set
\widehat{S}
might still be arbitrarily large.
So we need to make one more step: we observe that an inclusion-wise minimal subset
Q
of
\widehat{S}
that still can distinguish between the three types of vertices in
I\setminus S
is of size bounded by a function of
k
, as otherwise we could again extract from
F
a large induced matching whose every edge intersects
X_{t}
.
Thus we can guess every candidate for
Q
.

From the argument above it follows that the triple
(S,I,Q)
can be exhaustively guessed in a polynomial number of ways. Furthermore, thanks to the properties of
Q
, we can uniquely extract
L(F)\cap X_{t}
and
T(F)\cap X_{t}
from
I
.
This way we can compute
Z
, i.e., the intersection of
F
with
X_{t}
.

In order to obtain
\pi
, we can exhaustively guess the partition
\widehat{\pi}
of
S\cup Q
that corresponds to the components of
F
restricted to the subgraph of
G
induced by the bags in the subtree rooted at
t
. Here we use the fact that the size of
S\cup Q
is bounded by a function of
k
, i.e., a constant.
From
\widehat{\pi}
we can uniquely reconstruct
\pi
, as each vertex in
L(F)\cap X_{t}
has a neighbor in
S\cup Q
, and each vertex in
T(F)
forms a separate component.
This completes the sketch of proof of
Lemma

2.2
.

2.2
Independent packings of small subgraphs.

Let
\mathcal{H}=\{H_{j}\}_{j\in J}
be a family of connected subgraphs of a graph
G
.
By
G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}]
we denote the graph with vertex set
J
, where two distinct vertices
j,j^{\prime}\in J
are adjacent if
H_{j}
and
H_{j^{\prime}}
have a vertex in common or there is an edge with one endpoint in
H_{j}
and the other in
H_{j^{\prime}}
.
3
3

3

This extends the notation
G^{\circ}
used in
[
37
]
for the graph
G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}]
in the particular case when
\mathcal{H}
is the set of all non-null connected subgraphs of
G
.

Such a construction was considered by Cameron and Hell in
[
25
]
, who focused on the particular case when
\mathcal{H}
is the set of all subgraphs of
G
isomorphic to a member of a fixed family
\mathcal{F}
of connected graphs; they showed that if
G
is a chordal graph, then so is
G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}]
.
Dallard et al.
[
32
]
generalized this result by showing that
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}])\leqslant\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G)
.
Other graph classes closed under taking
G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}]
are classes of graphs excluding long induced paths or long induced cycles; see Gartland et al.
[
38
]
.
We show that an analogous statement holds for graphs with bounded induced matching treewidth.

Lemma 2.3

(A part of the statement of
Lemma

5.3
)
.

Let
G
be a graph and let
\mathcal{H}
be a set of connected non-null subgraphs of
G
.
Then
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}])\leqslant\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G)
.

Lemma

2.3
yields several algorithmic corollaries.
First, notice that the
MWIS
problem on
G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}]
(with some weight function defined on
J
) corresponds to the problem of packing pairwise disjoint and nonadjacent graphs from
\mathcal{H}
, called
Max Weight Independent Packing
. Thus, combining
Lemma

5.3
with
Theorem

1.1
,
we show that
Max Weight Independent Packing
in classes of bounded induced matching treewidth can be solved in time polynomial in the size of
G
and
\mathcal{H}
.
When
\mathcal{H}
is the set of all subgraphs of
G
isomorphic to a member of a fixed family
\mathcal{F}
of connected graphs, then
Max Weight Independent Packing
coincides with the
Max Weight Independent
\mathcal{F}
-Packing
problem studied in Dallard et al.
[
32
]
.
This latter problem in turn generalizes several problems studied in the literature:

•

the
MWIS
problem, which corresponds to the case
\mathcal{F}=\{K_{1}\}
,

•

the
Max Weight Induced Matching
problem (see, e.g.,
[
6
,
60
]
), which corresponds to the case
\mathcal{F}=\{K_{2}\}
,

•

the
Dissociation Set
problem (see, e.g.,
[
72
,
70
,
58
]
), which corresponds to the case when
\mathcal{F}=\{K_{1},K_{2}\}
and the weight function assigns to each subgraph
H_{j}
the weight equal to
|V(H_{j})|
,

•

the
k
-Separator
problem (see, e.g.,
[
48
,
49
,
11
]
), which corresponds to the case when
\mathcal{F}
contains all connected graphs with at most
k
vertices, the graph
G
is equipped with a vertex weight function
\mathfrak{w}:V(G)\to\mathbb{Q}_{+}
, and the weight function on
\mathcal{H}
assigns to each subgraph
H_{j}
the weight equal to
\sum_{x\in V(H_{j})}\mathfrak{w}(x)
.

Observe that
Max Weight Induced Forest
for a weighted graph
G
is equivalent to
Max Weight Independent Packing
for an instance
G
,
\mathcal{H}
(and
\mathfrak{w}
), where
\mathcal{H}
consists of all induced subtrees of
G
(and the weight of
H_{j}
is the total weight of the vertices in
H_{j}
).
Despite that, we note that the combination of
Lemma

2.3
and
Theorem

1.1
cannot be used to solve
Max Weight Induced Forest
in polynomial time. Indeed, the size of
\mathcal{H}
is not bounded by a polynomial function of
|V(G)|
.

However,
Lemma

2.3
might still be of use in this context.
As already observed by Gartland et al.
[
38
]
, we can sacrifice a small part of the optimal solution to break it into constant-size parts, which can then be handled efficiently.
Indeed, for each
\epsilon>0
and any forest
F
we can remove an
\epsilon
-fraction of the vertices of
F
so that the remaining components are of constant size (where this constant depends on
\epsilon
but not on
F
).
This approach can be used to obtain a PTAS for
Max Induced Forest
(the unweighted variant of
Max Weight Induced Forest
).
Actually, this reasoning goes far beyond trees and can be used to pack large induced subgraphs from any
weakly hyperfinite
class; see
Section

5
for the definition.
In particular, we obtain the following result which provides further evidence for
1.2
.

Corollary 2.4
.

For every fixed
k,r\in\mathbb{N}
and
\epsilon>0
, given a graph
G
with induced matching treewidth at most
k
,
in polynomial time we can find a set
F\subseteq V(G)
such that:

1.

\mathrm{tw}(G[F])\leqslant r
,

2.

the size of
F
is at least
(1-\epsilon)\textsf{OPT}
, where
OPT
is the size of a largest set satisfying the first condition.

Note that the problem addressed in
Corollary

2.4
is a special case of

(r,\psi)
-
MWIS
obtained by taking
\psi
to be any formula satisfied by all graphs and
\mathfrak{w}
to be a uniform weight function.

Next, we generalize the polynomial-time solvability of the
Max Weight Independent Packing
problem for graphs of bounded induced matching treewidth to
Max Weight Distance-
d
Packing
: the problem of packing subgraphs at distance
d
, for all even positive integers
d
.
The case
d=2
is precisely
Max Weight Independent Packing
.
We remark that unless
\textsf{P}=\textsf{NP}
, this result cannot be generalized to odd values of
d
, since (as shown by Eto, Guo, and Miyano
[
35
]
), the distance-
3
variant of
Max Independent Set
problem is
NP
-hard for chordal graphs, which have induced matching treewidth (and even tree-independence number) at most one.

This extension again follows from a structural observation that may be of interest on its own.
Given a graph
G
and a positive integer
k
, we denote by
G^{k}
the
k
-th power
of
G
, that is, the graph obtained from the graph
G
by adding to it all edges between pairs of distinct vertices at distance at most
k
.
We show that for any graph
G
and any positive integer
k
, the induced matching treewidth of the graph
G^{k+2}
cannot exceed that of the graph
G^{k}
, and the same relationship holds for the tree-independence number.
This is a significant generalization of a result of Duchet
[
34
]
, who proved an analogous result for graphs with tree-independence number at most one: if
G^{k}
is chordal, then so is
G^{k+2}
.
As a consequence, we obtain that for every positive integer
k
, the class of graphs with induced matching treewidth (resp. tree-independence number) at most
k
is closed under taking odd powers, which generalizes the analogous result for the class of chordal graphs proved by Balakrishnan and Paulraja
[
9
]
.
We complement these results by observing that the class of even powers of chordal graphs (for any fixed power) is not contained in any nontrivial hereditary graph class.
This implies in particular that any such class has unbounded tree-independence number and induced matching treewidth.

2.3
Solving
(r,\psi)
-MWIS for bounded tree-independence number.

Finally, we show that
(r,\psi)
-
MWIS
can be solved in polynomial time for graphs of bounded tree-independence number.
As shown by Dallard et al.
[
30
]
, many natural graph classes fall into this category. Furthermore, they all have bounded induced matching treewidth.

Actually, we show tractability of a more general problem, where instead of asking for a subgraph of bounded treewidth, we ask for a subgraph of clique number bounded by
r
; let us call this variant
(\omega\leqslant r,\psi)
-
MWIS
.
We remark that
(\omega\leqslant r,\psi)
-
MWIS
is a generalization of
(r-1,\psi)
-
MWIS
, as every graph of treewidth at most
r-1
has clique number at most
r
and the property of being of bounded treewidth can be expressed in
\mathsf{CMSO}_{2}

[
38
, Lemma 10]
.
On the other hand, there are natural classes of graphs of bounded clique number and unbounded treewidth, e.g., bipartite graphs or planar graphs.
However, one can observe that for graphs of bounded tree-independence number, treewidth is upper-bounded by a function of the clique number (see
[
32
]
); this phenomenon is called
(\mathrm{tw},\omega)
-boundedness
in the literature (see, e.g.,
[
31
]
).
Thus we conclude that in classes of bounded tree-independence number, both
(\omega\leqslant r,\psi)
-
MWIS
and
(r,\psi)
-
MWIS
formalisms describe the same family of problems.

Theorem 2.5

(Simplified statement of
Theorem

6.3
)
.

For every fixed
k,r
and a
\mathsf{CMSO}_{2}
formula
\psi
,
the
(\omega\leqslant r,\psi)
-
MWIS
for graphs with tree-independence number at most
k
can be solved in polynomial time.

Some examples of problems that can be solved in polynomial time with this approach include:

•

finding a largest induced planar subgraph (which is equivalent to
Planarization

[
43
,
61
]
),

•

finding a largest induced odd cactus (which is equivalent to
Even Cycle Transversal

[
59
,
54
,
12
]
),

•

finding the maximum number of pairwise disjoint and non-adjacent cycles (for this problem we need a slightly stronger variant of
Theorem

2.5
that is shown in
Section

6
).

We point out that the bound on the clique number of the sought-for subgraph must be constant.
Indeed, the property of being a clique is easily expressible in
\mathsf{CMSO}_{2}
, but
Max Clique
is
NP
-hard for graphs with tree-independence number 2.

2.4
Complexity of computing induced matching treewidth and bounds

We observe that known relations between the induced matching treewidth the tree-independence number
[
71
]
together with known results on tree-independence number
[
32
,
29
]
and independence number
[
73
]
imply several hardness results related to exact and approximate computation of the induced matching treewidth.
In particular, for every constant
k\geqslant 4
, it is
NP
-complete to determine whether
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G)\leqslant k
for a graph
G
(see
Theorem

7.1
for the precise statement of the results).
While the complexity of recognizing graphs with induced matching treewidth
k
for
k\in\{2,3\}
remains open, we then show that the problem is solvable in polynomial time for
k=1
.
To this end, we first prove that induced matching treewidth is monotone under induced minors, that is, it cannot decrease upon deleting a vertex or contracting an edge.
Then, we use a characterization of graphs
G
such that the square of the line graph of
G
is chordal (see
[
67
]
) to characterize the class of graphs with induced matching treewidth at most
1
in terms of a finite family of forbidden induced minors.
This characterization implies that the family of forbidden induced
subgraphs
for this class is very restricted, containing only finitely many graphs except for cycles of length at least
6
, which immediately leads to a polynomial-time recognition algorithm.

We then consider the behavior of induced matching treewidth for graphs with bounded degree, in particular, its relation to tree-independence number and treewidth.
While any graph class with unbounded induced matching treewidth is necessary of unbounded tree-independence number and hence of unbounded treewidth, it is known that the converse implications hold in the absence of a fixed complete bipartite graph as a subgraph (see p.
7.5
for details).
In particular, for any class of graphs with bounded maximum degree, all the aforementioned parameters (induced matching treewidth, tree-independence number, and treewidth) are all equivalent to each other, in the sense that they are either all bounded or all unbounded.
We strengthen this result by showing that for any class of graphs with bounded maximum degree, the three parameters are in fact linearly related to each other (see
Theorems

7.6
and
7.7
).

In conclusion, we consider two families of graphs with unbounded maximum degree.
We give a lower bound on the induced matching treewidth of hypercube graphs and identify a family of
P_{4}
-free graphs with unbounded induced matching treewidth.

Organization of the paper

In
Section

3
, we provide the necessary preliminaries.
In
Section

4
, we prove
Theorem

2.1
.
In
Section

5
, we show
Lemma

5.3
and discuss its algorithmic consequences, in particular,
Corollary

2.4
.
In
Section

6
, we show
Theorem

2.5
and its strengthened variant.
In
Section

7
we initiate a systematic study of induced matching treewidth, by showing several computational results and bounds.
The paper is concluded with pointing out some directions for further research in
Section

8
.

3
Preliminaries

For an integer
n
, by
[n]
we denote the set
\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
Let
G
be a graph.
For a set
X\subseteq V(G)
, by
G[X]
we denote the subgraph of
G
induced by
X
.
If the graph
G
is clear from the context, we will identify induced subgraphs with their vertex sets.

For a vertex
v
of
G
, by
N(v)
we denote the set of neighbors of
v
.
For a set
X\subseteq V(G)
, by
N(X)
we denote the set
\left(\bigcup_{v\in X}N(v)\right)\setminus X
, and by
N[X]
we denote the set
N(X)\cup X
.
For a vertex
v
of
G
, we write
N[v]
for
N[\{v\}]
.

Let
X,Y
be disjoint subsets of
V(G)
.
We say that they are
non-adjacent
if there is no edge with one endpoint in
X
and the other in
Y
.
For
k\in\{1,2\}
, the set
Y

k
-dominates

X
if each vertex from
X
has at least
k
neighbors in
Y
.

A
clique
in a graph
G
is a set of pairwise adjacent vertices; an
independent set
is a set of pairwise non-adjacent vertices.
The
clique number
and the
independence number
of
G
, denoted respectively by
\omega(G)
and
\alpha(G)
, are defined as the maximum cardinality of a clique, resp. independent set, in
G
.

A
matching
in a graph
G
is a set
M
of pairwise disjoint edges.
If, in addition, no two vertices from different edges in the matching
M
are adjacent, then the matching
M
is an
induced matching
in
G
.
An edge
touches
a set
X\subseteq V(G)
if it has at least one endpoint in
X
.
A set
M
of edges
touches

X
if every edge from
M
touches
X
.

The
distance
between two vertices
u
and
v
in
G
is denoted by
\textsf{dist}_{G}(u,v)
and defined as the length of a shortest path between
u
and
v
(or
\infty
if there is no
u
-
v
-path in
G
).
Given a positive integer
k
, the
k
-th power
of
G
is the graph
G^{k}
with vertex set
V(G)
, in which two distinct vertices
u
and
v
are adjacent if and only if
\textsf{dist}_{G}(u,v)\leqslant k
. Note that
G^{1}=G
.

Let
\mathfrak{w}\colon V(G)\to\mathbb{Q}_{+}
be a weight function.
For a set
X\subseteq V(G)
, by
\mathfrak{w}(X)
we denote
\sum_{v\in X}\mathfrak{w}(v)
.
In particular,
\mathfrak{w}(\emptyset)=0
.

Tree decompositions.

A
tree decomposition
of a graph
G
is a pair
\mathcal{T}=(T,\{X_{t}\}_{t\in V(T)})
where
T
is a tree and every node
t
of
T
is assigned a vertex subset
X_{t}\subseteq V(G)
called a
bag
such that the following conditions are satisfied: every vertex is in at least one bag, for every edge
uv\in E(G)
there exists a node
t\in V(T)
such that
X_{t}
contains both
u
and
v
, and for every vertex
u\in V(G)
the subgraph of
T
induced by the set
\{t\in V(T):u\in X_{t}\}
is connected (that is, a tree).

Usually we consider tree decompositions to be rooted, and the root is denoted by
\mathsf{root}(T)
.
For a node
t
of
T
, by
T_{t}
we denote the subtree of
T
rooted at
t
, and by
G_{t}
we denote the subgraph of
G
induced by
V_{t}\coloneqq\bigcup_{t^{\prime}\in V(T_{t})}X_{t^{\prime}}
.
A rooted tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}=(T,\{X_{t}\}_{t\in V(T)})
of a graph
G
is
nice

if each node
t
is of one of the following types:

leaf

a leaf in
T
such that
X_{t}=\emptyset
,

introduce

an inner node with a single child
t^{\prime}
such that
X_{t}=X_{t^{\prime}}\cup\{v\}
for some
v\in V(G)\setminus X_{t^{\prime}}
,

forget

an inner node with a single child
t^{\prime}
such that
X_{t}=X_{t^{\prime}}\setminus\{v\}
for some
v\in X_{t^{\prime}}
,

join

an inner node with exactly two children
t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime}
, such that
X_{t}=X_{t^{\prime}}=X_{t^{\prime\prime}}
,

and
X_{\mathsf{root}(T)}=\emptyset
.
It is well known that given a tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}=(T,\{X_{t}\}_{t\in V(T)})
of
G
, one can obtain in time
\mathcal{O}(|V(G)|^{2}|V(T)|)
a nice tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}=(T^{\prime},\{X^{\prime}_{t}\}_{t\in V(T^{\prime})})
of
G
with at most
|V(G)|\cdot|V(T)|
nodes and whose every bag is contained in some bag of
\mathcal{T}
(see, e.g., the textbook
[
28
]
, or
[
32
]
for a treatment focused on the independence number).

Tree-independence number and induced matching treewidth.

Consider a graph
G
and a tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}=(T,\{X_{t}\}_{t\in V(T)})
of
G
.
The
independence number
of
\mathcal{T}
, denoted by
\alpha_{G}(\mathcal{T})
, is defined as follows:

\alpha_{G}(\mathcal{T})=\max_{t\in V(T)}\alpha(G[X_{t}])\,.

The
tree-independence number
of
G
, denoted by
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G)
, is the minimum independence number among all possible tree decompositions of
G
.
A graph
G
is chordal if and only if
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G)\leqslant 1
(see
[
32
]
).

Let again
G
be a graph and
\mathcal{T}=(T,\{X_{t}\}_{t\in V(T)})
be its tree decomposition.
Let

\mu_{G}(\mathcal{T})=\max\left\{|M|\colon\textnormal{ $M$ is an induced matching in $G$ that touches a bag of }\mathcal{T}\right\}\,.

The
induced matching treewidth
of
G
, denoted by
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G)
, is the minimum value of
\mu_{G}(\mathcal{T})
among all possible tree decompositions
\mathcal{T}
of
G
. Recall that every graph
G
satisfies
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G)\leqslant\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G)
.

Note that if
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
are tree decompositions such that every bag of
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
is contained in some bag of
\mathcal{T}
(in particular, if
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
is a nice tree decomposition obtained from
\mathcal{T}
), then we have
\alpha_{G}(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})\leqslant\alpha_{G}(\mathcal{T})
and
\mu_{G}(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})\leqslant\mu_{G}(\mathcal{T})
.
If the graph
G
is clear from the context, we simply write
\alpha(\mathcal{T})
and
\mu(\mathcal{T})
for
\alpha_{G}(\mathcal{T})
and
\mu_{G}(\mathcal{T})
, respectively.

We now recall two results by Yolov from
[
71
]
, expressing each of these two parameters in terms of the value of the other parameter on a derived graph.
Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph.
We denote by
L^{2}(G)
the
square of the line graph
of
G
, that is, the graph with vertex set
E
in which two distinct vertices
e,f\in E
are adjacent if and only if the subgraph of
G
induced by the endpoints of
e
and
f
is connected.
Furthermore, by
G\odot K_{1}
we denote the
corona
of
G
, that is, the graph obtained from
G
by adding a pendant edge to each vertex.
Formally,
V(G\odot K_{1})=V\cup V^{\prime}

where
V^{\prime}=\{v^{\prime}:v\in V\}
is a set of
|V|
new vertices, and
E(G\odot K_{1})=E\cup\{vv^{\prime}:v\in V\}
.

Proposition 3.1

(Yolov
[
71
]
)
.

For every graph
G
, it holds that

\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G)=\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(L^{2}(G))\quad\quad\textrm{and}\quad\quad\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G)=\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G\odot K_{1})\,.

Furthermore, given a graph with bounded tree-independence number or bounded induced matching treewidth, we can compute in polynomial time a tree decomposition with bounded
\alpha(\mathcal{T})
or
\mu(\mathcal{T})
, respectively.
Dallard et al. showed in
[
29
]
that there is an algorithm that, given an
n
-vertex graph
G
and an integer
k
, in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k^{2})}n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
either outputs a tree decomposition of
G
with with independence number at most
8k
, or determines that the tree-independence number of
G
is larger than
k
.
This improves on an earlier analogous result of Yolov
[
71
]
giving an algorithm with running time
n^{\mathcal{O}(k^{3})}
and a bound on the independence number of
\mathcal{O}(k^{3})
.
A consequence of the aforementioned result is the following.

Theorem 3.2

(Dallard et al.
[
29
]
)
.

Let
k
be a positive integer and let
G
be an
n
-vertex graph satisfying
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G)\leqslant k
.
Then, in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
we can obtain a tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}
of
G
such that
\alpha(\mathcal{T})\leqslant 8k
.

Combining
Theorem

3.2
with
Proposition

3.1
(and its constructive proof) yields a similar result for the induced matching treewidth.

Theorem 3.3
.

Let
k
be a positive integer and let
G
be an
n
-vertex graph satisfying
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G)\leqslant k
.
Then, in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
we can obtain a tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}
of
G
such that
\mu(\mathcal{T})\leqslant 8k
.

Proof.

By
Proposition

3.1
,
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(L^{2}(G))=\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G)\leqslant k
.
Note that the graph
L^{2}(G)
has
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
vertices and can be computed in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.
Applying
Theorem

3.2
to the graph
L^{2}(G)
, we obtain in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
a tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
of
L^{2}(G)
such that
\alpha(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})\leqslant 8k
.
Following the proof of
[
71
, Theorem 4.2]
(establishing
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G)=\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(L^{2}(G))
), this tree decomposition can be turned in polynomial time into a tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}
of
G
such that
\mu(\mathcal{T})\leqslant 8k
.
∎

The following property of tree decompositions with bounded induced matching treewidth allows us to exploit them algorithmically, in order to solve the
MWIS
problem.

Theorem 3.4

(Yolov
[
71
]
)
.

Let
k
be a positive integer, let
G
be an
n
-vertex graph, and let
\mathcal{T}=(T,\{X_{t}\}_{t\in V(T)})
be a tree decomposition of
G
such that
\mu(\mathcal{T})\leqslant k
.
Then for every
t\in V(T)
, in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
one can enumerate a family
\mathbb{I}_{t}
of at most
n^{2k+2}
subsets of
X_{t}
with the following property:
For any maximal independent set
I
of
G
, the set
I\cap X_{t}
belongs to
\mathbb{I}_{t}
.

We conclude this section with a short alternative proof of
Theorem

3.4
.
We include it for two reasons.
First, we believe it is easier to follow (at least in the context of the current paper), as the terminology and notation of
[
71
]
is quite heavy and very different from ours.
Second, it serves as a warm-up for the main combinatorial result of
Section

4
, i.e.,
Lemma

4.1
, which uses the same basic idea, but is significantly more complicated.
We remark that the presented argument gives a slightly worse bound on the size of
\mathbb{I}_{t}
; we do not attempt to optimize it, rather aiming for simplicity.

Proof of
Theorem

3.4
(with the bound of
n^{3k}
on the size of
\mathbb{I}_{t}
).

Fix some
t\in V(T)
.
Define

\mathbb{I}^{*}=\{J\colon\exists\text{ a maximal independent set $I$ in $G$ s.t.~$I\cap X_{t}=J$}\}.

We will show that in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
we can enumerate a set
\mathbb{I}_{t}
of size at most
n^{3k}
that contains
\mathbb{I}^{*}
.

Fix some
J\in\mathbb{I}^{*}
and let
I
be a maximal independent set in
G
such that
I\cap X_{t}=J
.
There exists some set
J^{\prime}
that is a maximal independent set in
G[X_{t}]
and contains
J
.
Notice that every vertex of
J^{\prime}\setminus J
is adjacent to some vertex in
I\cap N(X_{t})
. Let
Q
be an inclusion-wise minimal subset of
I\cap N(X_{t})
such that every vertex from
J^{\prime}\setminus J
has a neighbor in
Q
.

Notice that by the minimality of
Q
each element
q\in Q
has a
private neighbor

r_{q}\in J^{\prime}\setminus J
, i.e.,
qr_{q^{\prime}}
is an edge if and only if
q=q^{\prime}
. Furthermore, as each of
Q
and
J^{\prime}\setminus J
is independent, we observe that
the vertices
\bigcup_{q\in Q}\{q,r_{q}\}
induce in
G
a matching of size
|Q|
that touches
X_{t}
.
Consequently,
|Q|\leqslant k
.
Now observe that the pair
(J^{\prime},Q)
uniquely determines
J
as
J=J^{\prime}\setminus N(Q)
.

By a result of Alekseev
[
7
, Corollary 1]
, if a graph
H
has no induced matching of size larger than
k
, then the number of maximal independent sets in
H
is at most
|V(H)|^{2k}
. As
G[X_{t}]
is such a graph, we conclude that the number of candidates for
J^{\prime}
is at most
n^{2k}
.
Furthermore, maximal independent sets can be enumerated with polynomial delay
[
69
]
, so we can enumerate all candidates for
J^{\prime}
in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
.
On the other hand, there are clearly at most
n^{k}
candidates for
Q
and they can be enumerated in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
. This completes the proof.
∎

4
Solving
Max Weight Induced Forest

Let
F
be an induced forest in
G
and
\mathcal{T}=(T,\{X_{t}\}_{t\in V(T)})
be a tree decomposition of
G
.
The
signature of
F
at
t\in V(T)
is a pair
(Z,\pi)
, such that:

1.

Z=F\cap X_{t}
, and

2.

\pi
is the partition of
Z
such that
z_{1},z_{2}\in Z
are in the same block of
\pi
if and only if there is a
z_{1}
-
z_{2}
-path in
F\cap V_{t}
.

In other words,
\pi
corresponds to connected components of the subgraph of
F
induced by
V_{t}
.

Note that, given a tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}
with
\mu(\mathcal{T})\leqslant k
, we cannot bound the number of possible signatures of any induced forest at each bag of
\mathcal{T}
by a function of
k
. Indeed, it might happen that a bag induces an independent set of size linear in
n=|V(G)|
; then, every subset of such a bag forms an induced forest in
G
. However, this is no longer the case if we talk about
inclusion-wise maximal
induced forests; note that we can safely assume that every optimal solution is maximal.
The main combinatorial insight used in the proof of
Theorem

2.1
is the following lemma, which is an analogue of
Theorem

3.4
for the
Max Weight Induced Forest
problem.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
k
be a fixed integer.
Let
G
be an
n
-vertex graph and let
\mathcal{T}=(T,\{X_{t}\}_{t\in V(T)})
be a tree decomposition of
G
satisfying
\mu(\mathcal{T})\leqslant k
.
Then, for each
t\in V(T)
there is a set
\mathbb{F}_{t}
of size at most
(12k)^{12k}\cdot n^{14k+2}
with the following property:
For every maximal induced forest
F
of
G
, the signature of
F
at
t
is in
\mathbb{F}_{t}
.
Furthermore,
\mathbb{F}_{t}
can be enumerated in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
.

Equipped with
Lemma

4.1
, we can basically mimic the textbook algorithm for
Max Weight Induced Forest

[
28
]
.
This is encapsulated in the following lemma.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
k
be a fixed integer.
Let
G
be an
n
-vertex graph equipped with a vertex weight function
\mathfrak{w}
and a tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}=(T,\{X_{t}\}_{t\in V(T)})
satisfying
\mu(\mathcal{T})\leqslant k
.
Assume that for each
t\in V(T)
we are given a set
\mathbb{F}_{t}
as in
Lemma

4.1
.
Then, we can solve the
Max Weight Induced Forest
problem in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}\cdot|V(T)|^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.

Let us postpone the proofs of
Lemmas

4.1
and
4.2
and now let us show that they indeed imply
Theorem

2.1
.
Let
G
be a graph satisfying
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G)\leqslant k
, equipped with a weight function
\mathfrak{w}\colon V(G)\to\mathbb{Q}_{+}
.
We call the algorithm from
Theorem

3.3
to obtain a tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
with
\mu(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})=\mathcal{O}(k)
.
The running time of this algorithm is
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
.
In polynomial time we can transform
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
into a nice tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}=(T,\{X_{t}\}_{t\in V(T)})
with
\mu(\mathcal{T})\leqslant\mu(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})
.
Define
k^{\prime}\coloneqq\mu(\mathcal{T})
.

Next, for each
t\in V(T)
we use
Lemma

4.1
to compute the set
\mathbb{F}_{t}
; this takes total time
n^{\mathcal{O}(k^{\prime})}
.
Finally, we call
Lemma

4.2
in order to solve
Max Weight Induced Forest
with instance
(G,\mathfrak{w})
. The overall running time is
n^{\mathcal{O}(k^{\prime})}={n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}}
.

4.1
Proof of
Lemma

4.1

The proof is based on a structural analysis of potential signatures of an optimal solution at a node
t
of
\mathcal{T}
.

What lives in a forest.

Let
F
be a forest.
A one-vertex component of
F
is called
trivial
, each other component is
nontrivial
.
The set of vertices of trivial components of
F
is denoted by
T(F)
.
Let
L(F)
consist of
leaves
of
F
, i.e., the vertices of degree 1, with the exception that for each component
C
isomorphic to
K_{2}
we include in
L(F)
exactly one vertex from
C
, breaking ties arbitrarily.
4
4

4

For example, by fixing an arbitrary linear order on
V(G)
and selecting as the vertex in
V(C)\cap L(F)
the first vertex of
C
in the ordering.

Skeletons of maximal induced forests.

Let
G
be a graph,
\mathcal{T}=(T,\{X_{t}\}_{t\in V(T)})
be a tree decomposition of
G
with
\mu(\mathcal{T})\leqslant k
, and
t\in V(T)
.
Let
F
be a maximal induced forest in
G
.

First, let us show that, even though

N[X_{t}]
that intersect
X_{t}
.
Now define
S_{2}
to be the forest obtained from
S_{1}
by (i) repeatedly contracting all edges not intersecting
X_{t}
, and then (ii) removing leaves that are not in
X_{t}
.
Observe that
S\subseteq S_{2}
and every edge of
S_{2}
touches
X_{t}
.
Each vertex that was removed in step (ii) has a unique neighbor in
S_{2}
; denote the set of these neighbors by
U
.

A component of
S_{2}
is
small
if it has at most two vertices.
Let
a
be the number of small components of
S_{2}
and let
S_{3}
be obtained from
S_{2}
by removing all small components.
A
thread
in
S_{3}
is a maximal path with all internal vertices of degree 2.
Consider a thread in
S_{3}
with consecutive vertices
v_{0},v_{1},\ldots,v_{\ell}
, where
v_{0}
and
v_{\ell}
are of degree other than 2 and
\ell\geqslant 5
.
Let
q=\lfloor(\ell-5)/3\rfloor
.
We mark edges
v_{3i+2},v_{3i+3}
for
i\in[0,q]
.
Finally, we remove all vertices
v_{j}
for
j\in[2,\ldots,3q+4]
and add an edge between
v_{1}
and
v_{3q+5}
; note that
3q+5\leqslant\ell
.
In this step we marked
q+1
edges and removed
3(q+1)
vertices.
The marked edges are pairwise non-adjacent (i.e., they form an induced matching).
Furthermore, they are not incident with vertices of degree other than 2 in
S_{3}
.
Let
S_{4}
be obtained from
S_{3}
by the application of the step above to each thread of
S_{3}
.
Define
b\coloneqq(|S_{3}|-|S_{4}|)/3
.

Finally, define
c
to be the number of leaves in
S_{4}
.
Note that the number of vertices of degree at least 3 in
S_{4}
is at most
c
,
thus
S_{4}
has at most
2c-1
threads,
each with at most 3 internal vertices.
Consequently,
S_{4}
has at most
2c+(2c-1)\cdot 3\leqslant 8c
vertices.

Summing up, we obtain that

|S|\leqslant|S_{2}|\leqslant 2a+3b+8c.

(1)

Let
A
be a set of size
a
containing one vertex per each small component of
S_{2}
; note that
A\subseteq X_{t}
.

of

.
Note that for each small component
H
of
S_{2}
, the unique component of
S_{1}
that contains

intersects
X_{t}
in no component other than
H
.
Thus
M_{A}=\{vu(v)~|~v\in A\}
is an induced matching in
G
of size
a
that touches
X_{t}
.

Let
B
be the set of all edges that were marked while obtaining
S_{4}
from
S_{3}
.
Note that they induce a matching
M_{B}
in
G
of size
b
that touches
X_{t}
.

Finally, let
C
be the set of leaves of
S_{4}
.
Recall that
C\subseteq X_{t}
and the vertices from
C
are also leaves in
S_{2}
. Furthermore, as
S_{4}
does not contain small components,
the vertices in
C
form an independent set in
G
.
For each
v\in C
, define a neighbor
u(v)
of
v
as follows.
If

N(X_{t})\cap N(v)
that was deleted in step (ii).
We observe that
M_{C}\coloneqq\{vu(v)~|~v\in C\}
is an induced matching in
G
of size
c
that touches
X_{t}
.

We claim that
M=M_{A}\cup M_{B}\cup M_{C}
is an induced matching in
G
of size
a+b+c
.
It is straightforward to verify that
M
is a matching of the desired size that touches
X_{t}
.
The only nontrivial thing to verify is that there are no edges between the vertices inducing
M_{B}
and
M_{C}
. However, recall that none of the endpoints of edges marked in the process of creating
S_{4}
from
S_{3}
is adjacent to a vertex of degree other than 2, so
M
is indeed an induced matching.

We know that
a+b+c=|M|\leqslant k
, which, combined with (
1
), completes the proof.

\triangleleft

Let us remark that we did not make too much effort to optimize the multiplicative constant in
4.2.1
.
We decided to aim for simplicity as the constant is, anyway, hidden in the
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
notation in the final running time.

Identifying the impostors.

Now we need to understand the set
\dddot{\text{\textleaf}}({F})\cap X_{t}
; recall that it might be arbitrarily large.
By
Theorem

3.4
we know that the number of ways
maximal
independents sets of
G
might intersect
X_{t}
is polynomially bounded.
We are going to exploit this.
Since
\dddot{\text{\textleaf}}({F})
is an independent set, it is contained in some maximal independent set
I^{*}(F)
in
G
, and
I^{*}(F)\cap X_{t}\in\mathbb{I}_{t}
.
Consequently, the number of possible choices for
I\coloneqq I^{*}(F)\cap X_{t}
is bounded by
n^{2k+2}
.

However, we still need to be able to filter out the vertices from
I\setminus\dddot{\text{\textleaf}}({F})
using some information that can be stored and processed efficiently.
We will show that this is indeed possible.

Consider a vertex
v\in I\setminus\dddot{\text{\textleaf}}({F})
.
By the maximality of
F
, there are two possible reasons why
v\notin\dddot{\text{\textleaf}}({F})
:

.

It will also be convenient to distinguish vertices from
T(F)
and vertices from
L(F)
.
Note that the latter ones have
exactly
one neighbor in
\widehat{S}
, while the former ones have no neighbors in
\widehat{S}
.
Summing up, we observe that
\widehat{S}

(Q1)

2-dominates all impostors and does not 2-dominate any vertex from
\dddot{\text{\textleaf}}({F})\cap X_{t}
,

(Q2)

1-dominates all vertices from
L(F)\cap X_{t}
,

(Q3)

is non-adjacent to
T(F)\cap X_{t}
.

Let
Q\subseteq\widehat{S}
be an inclusion-wise minimal subset of
\widehat{S}
that still satisfies (Q1), (Q2), and (Q3).

Claim 4.2.2
.

|Q|\leqslant 4k
.

Proof of Claim .
Define
L\coloneqq L(F)\cap X_{t}
and let
A
be the set of impostors, i.e., vertices in
I\setminus F
(see
Figure

1
).

Figure 1:
Various subsets of
V(G)
relevant in the proof of
Lemma

4.1
.

Let
Q_{1}\subseteq Q
be a minimal set that 1-dominates
L\cup A
.
From the minimality of
Q_{1}
it follows that for every
q\in Q_{1}
there exists
m(q)\in L\cup A
such that
N(m(q))\cap Q_{1}=\{q\}
.
Clearly the set
M_{1}\coloneqq\{qm(q)~|~q\in Q_{1}\}
is a matching.
Furthermore, the only edges between the vertices of
\bigcup_{e\in M_{1}}e
that are not in
M_{1}
are between the vertices of
Q_{1}
.
As
Q_{1}
is a forest, it is in particular 2-colorable, hence, there exists an independent set
Q^{\prime}_{1}\subseteq Q_{1}
of size at least
|Q_{1}|/2
.
Hence,
M^{\prime}_{1}\coloneqq\{qm(q)~|~q\in Q^{\prime}_{1}\}
is an induced matching of size at least
|Q_{1}|/2
that touches
X_{t}
.

Now let
A^{\prime}
be the set of vertices from
A
that are not 2-dominated by
Q_{1}
.
We observe that the set
Q_{2}\coloneqq Q\setminus Q_{1}
1-dominates
A^{\prime}
and, by the minimality of
Q
, is minimal with this property.
Thus, similarly as in the previous paragraph we can conclude that there exists an induced matching of size at least
|Q_{2}|/2
that touches
X_{t}
.

As
Q_{1}\cup Q_{2}=Q
, one of these sets has at least
|Q|/2
vertices.
Thus, there exists an induced matching of size at least
|Q|/4
that touches
X_{t}
.
As this matching can have at most
k
edges, we conclude that
|Q|\leqslant 4k
.

\triangleleft

Construction of
\mathbb{F}_{t}
.

Let
S,I,Q
be the sets defined as above.
Furthermore, let
\widehat{\pi}
be the partition of
S\cup Q
corresponding to the connected components of
F\cap V_{t}
,
i.e., two vertices are in one block of
\widehat{\pi}
if and only if they are in the same connected component of
F\cap V_{t}
.

We claim that the quadruple
(S,I,Q,\widehat{\pi})
uniquely determines the signature
(Z,\pi)
of
F
at
t
.

Recall that
Z=F\cap }{\hbox{}\picture(2.48,0.0)(-1.24,-1.0)\roundcap\moveto(-.33,-.7)\curveto(-.3,-.6)(-.22,-.6)(-.19,-.75)\curveto(-.15,-.9)(-.38,-.9)(-.33,-.7)\strokepath\moveto(-.5,-.7)\curveto(-.45,-.6)(-.4,-.6)(-.35,-.7)\curveto(-.4,-1.2)(-.5,-1.2)(-.5,-.7)\strokepath\moveto(-.17,-.75)\curveto(-.13,-.65)(-.05,-.62)(-.02,-.75)\curveto(+.03,-.95)(-.22,-.95)(-.17,-.75)\strokepath\moveto(+.33,-.7)\curveto(+.3,-.6)(+.22,-.6)(+.19,-.75)\curveto(+.15,-.9)(+.38,-.9)(+.33,-.7)\strokepath\moveto(+.5,-.7)\curveto(+.45,-.6)(+.4,-.6)(+.35,-.7)\curveto(+.4,-1.2)(+.5,-1.2)(+.5,-.7)\strokepath\moveto(+.17,-.75)\curveto(+.13,-.65)(+.05,-.62)(+.02,-.75)\curveto(-.03,-.95)(+.22,-.95)(+.17,-.75)\strokepath\moveto(0,-.5)\curveto(-.1,-.5)(-.2,-.5)(-.15,-.4)\curveto(-.1,-.1)(.1,-.1)(.15,-.4)\curveto(.2,-.5)(.1,-.5)(0,-.5)\fillpath\moveto(-.15,0)\curveto(-.15,.1)(-.15,.21)(-.4,.2)\curveto(-.6,.19)(-.69,.1)(-.7,0)\curveto(-.71,-.1)(-.6,-.3)(-.5,-.3)\curveto(-.4,-.3)(-.15,-.2)(-.15,0)\fillpath\cbezier(0,1.333336)(-1,1.333336)(-1.2,0.5)(-1,0)\cbezier(-1,0)(-1.05,-.9)(-.5,-.3)(-.5,-.65)\moveto(+.15,0)\curveto(+.15,.1)(+.15,.21)(+.4,.2)\curveto(+.6,.19)(+.69,.1)(+.7,0)\curveto(+.71,-.1)(+.6,-.3)(+.5,-.3)\curveto(+.4,-.3)(+.15,-.2)(+.15,0)\fillpath\cbezier(0,1.333336)(+1,1.333336)(+1.2,0.5)(+1,0)\cbezier(+1,0)(+1.05,-.9)(+.5,-.3)(+.5,-.65)\endpicture}}({F})\cap X_{t}=S
by the definition of
S
.
Next,
L(F)\cap X_{t}
consists of these vertices from
I\setminus S
that are 1-dominated but not 2-dominated by
Q
.
Finally,
T(F)
are the vertices from
I\setminus S
that are non-adjacent to
Q
.
Note that the remaining vertices from
I\setminus S
, i.e., those that are 2-dominated by
Q
, are impostors.

Now let us argue how to obtain
\pi
from
\widehat{\pi}
.
We start with
\pi\coloneqq\widehat{\pi}
; we will modify this partition as follows.
First, recall that each
v\in L(F)\cap X_{t}
has exactly one neighbor
u
in
F
, and additionally
u\in Q
.
If
u\in V_{t}
, then
v
is in the same component of
F\cap V_{t}
as
u
, i.e., we insert
v
into the block of
\pi
containing
u
. If
u\notin V_{t}
, then
v
is an isolated vertex in
F\cap V_{t}
and thus we add a new block
\{v\}
to
\pi
.
Similarly, for each
v\in T(F)\cap X_{t}
, we add to
\pi
a new block
\{v\}
.
Finally, we remove from the blocks of
\pi
all vertices from
Q\setminus S
.
Note that the last step might result in creating an empty block; we remove such blocks.
This completes the construction of
\pi
.

From the description above it follows that
(Z,\pi)
is indeed the signature of
F
at
t
.
We can exhaustively guess
(S,I,Q,\widehat{\pi})
which results in at most

n^{8k}\cdot n^{2k+2}\cdot n^{4k}\cdot(12k)^{12k}=(12k)^{12k}\cdot n^{14k+2}

possibilities.
For each choice we construct in polynomial time one pair
(Z,\pi)
and insert it to
\mathbb{F}_{t}
.
Note that some pairs could be immediately discarded, e.g., if
Z
is not a forest, but this is not necessary so we do not do it.
This completes the proof of
Lemma

4.1
.

4.2
Proof of
Lemma

4.2

The proof of
Lemma

4.2
follows the textbook dynamic programming algorithm for
Max Weight Induced Forest
in bounded-treewidth graphs.
However, the restriction that we can only iterate on elements of
\mathbb{F}_{t}
for
t\in V(T)
adds some extra technical complicacy to the proof of correctness.

Filling the dynamic programming table.

We index a dynamic programming table
\mathsf{Tab}
by the nodes
t\in V(T)
and the pairs
(Z,\pi)\in\mathbb{F}_{t}
(recall that
Z\subseteq X_{t}
and
\pi
is a partition of
Z
). The intended meaning is as follows:

(
\star
)

The value of
\mathsf{Tab}_{t}[Z,\pi]
is the maximum weight of an induced forest in
V_{t}
with signature
(Z,\pi)
at
t
, or
-\infty
if such a forest does not exist.

Note that since
X_{\mathsf{root}(T)}=\emptyset
, the signature of
every
induced forest in
G
at
\mathsf{root}(T)
is
(\emptyset,\emptyset)
.
In particular,
(\emptyset,\emptyset)\in\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{root}(T)}
. Thus, if property (
\star
) is satisfied,
the value
\mathsf{Tab}_{\mathsf{root}(T)}[\emptyset,\emptyset]
is the weight of an optimal solution to
Max Weight Induced Forest
.

Let
t\in V(T)
be a node and consider an arbitrary
(Z,\pi)\in\mathbb{F}_{t}
.
Before we proceed to the detailed description, notice that in some cases we can immediately detect that
(Z,\pi)
is not a signature of any solution. In particular, this might happen if:

•

Z
contains a cycle, or

•

there are two adjacent vertices in
Z
that are in different blocks of
\pi
.

In these two cases we immediately set
\mathsf{Tab}_{t}[Z,\pi]=-\infty
.
So from now on assume that
Z
is an induced forest and
\pi

respects the components of
Z
, i.e., any two vertices in the same component of
Z
are in the same block of
\pi
.
How the value of
\mathsf{Tab}_{t}[Z,\pi]
is computed depends on the type of
t
.

Leaf node.

If
t
is a leaf, then
X_{t}=\emptyset
, thus the signature of every induced forest at
t
is
(\emptyset,\emptyset)
, so in particular
(\emptyset,\emptyset)\in\mathbb{F}_{t}
.
We set
\mathsf{Tab}_{t}[\emptyset,\emptyset]=0
.

For all other types of nodes the value of
\mathsf{Tab}_{t}[Z,\pi]
depends on the values stored in
\mathsf{Tab}
for children node(s).
In the description below we use the convention that the maximum over an empty set is
-\infty
.

Introduce node.

Let
t
be an introduce node,
t^{\prime}
be its child in
T
, and let
v
be the unique vertex in
X_{t}\setminus X_{t^{\prime}}
.

Case 1.
v\notin Z
.
We set
\mathsf{Tab}_{t}[Z,\pi]=\mathsf{Tab}_{t^{\prime}}[Z,\pi]
.

Case 2.
v\in Z
.

A partition
\pi^{\prime}
of
Z\setminus\{v\}
is
v
-good
if each vertex from
N(v)\cap Z
is in a distinct block of
\pi^{\prime}
.
For a
v
-good partition
\pi^{\prime}
of
Z\setminus\{v\}
, by
\pi^{\prime}\oplus v
we mean the partition obtained from
\pi^{\prime}
by first adding a new block
\{v\}
and then merging all blocks intersecting
N[v]
into a single one.
We set

\mathsf{Tab}_{t}[Z,\pi]=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}(Z\setminus\{v\},\pi^{\prime})\in\mathbb{F}_{t^{\prime}}\\
\text{ s.t. }\pi^{\prime}\text{ is $v$-good}\\
\text{and }\pi^{\prime}\oplus v=\pi\end{subarray}}\mathsf{Tab}_{t^{\prime}}[Z\setminus\{v\},\pi^{\prime}]+\mathfrak{w}(v).

(2)

Forget node.

Let
t
be a forget node,
t^{\prime}
be its child in
T
, and let
v
be the unique vertex in
X_{t^{\prime}}\setminus X_{t}
.

Let
\Sigma
be a set defined as follows.
First, we include
(Z,\pi)
into
\Sigma
.
Next, for each block
B
of
\pi
, we include to
\Sigma
the pair
(Z\cup\{v\},\pi_{B})
, where
\pi_{B}
is obtained from
\pi
by adding
v
to
B
.
Finally, we add to
\Sigma
the pair
(Z\cup\{v\},\pi_{\emptyset})
, where
\pi_{\emptyset}
is obtained from
\pi
by adding a new block
\{v\}
.
We set

\mathsf{Tab}_{t}[Z,\pi]=\max_{(Z^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})\in\Sigma\cap\mathbb{F}_{t^{\prime}}}\mathsf{Tab}_{t^{\prime}}[Z^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}].

(3)

Join node.

Let
t
be a join node, and let
t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime}
be its children. Recall that we have
X_{t}=X_{t^{\prime}}=X_{t^{\prime\prime}}
.

Consider two partitions
\pi^{\prime},\pi^{\prime\prime}
of
Z
that respect the components of
Z
. We define an auxiliary multigraph
\mathsf{Merge}(\pi^{\prime},\pi^{\prime\prime})
as follows. The vertices of
\mathsf{Merge}(\pi^{\prime},\pi^{\prime\prime})
are the components of
Z
.
We add an edge between two components if they are in the same block of
\pi^{\prime}
, and then similarly for
\pi^{\prime\prime}
; note that we might create parallel edges.
If
\mathsf{Merge}(\pi^{\prime},\pi^{\prime\prime})
is a simple forest (in particular, does not contain double edges), we define a partition
\pi^{\prime}\otimes\pi^{\prime\prime}
of
Z
as follows.
For each component
C
of
\mathsf{Merge}(\pi^{\prime},\pi^{\prime\prime})
, we add a new block to
\pi^{\prime}\otimes\pi^{\prime\prime}
containing the union of the vertex sets of the components of
Z
in
V(C)
.
If
\mathsf{Merge}(\pi^{\prime},\pi^{\prime\prime})
is not a forest, then
\pi^{\prime}\otimes\pi^{\prime\prime}
is undefined.

We set

\mathsf{Tab}_{t}[Z,\pi]=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}(Z,\pi^{\prime})\in\mathbb{F}_{t^{\prime}},\\
(Z,\pi^{\prime\prime})\in\mathbb{F}_{t^{\prime\prime}},\\
\text{s.t. }\pi^{\prime}\otimes\pi^{\prime\prime}=\pi\end{subarray}}\left(\mathsf{Tab}_{t^{\prime}}[Z,\pi^{\prime}]+\mathsf{Tab}_{t^{\prime\prime}}[Z,\pi^{\prime\prime}]\right)-\mathfrak{w}(Z).

(4)

Running time.

Recall that the number of nodes is polynomial in
n
, and the number of entries in
\mathsf{Tab}
for each node
t
is exactly
|\mathbb{F}_{t}|
, where
|\mathbb{F}_{t}|=n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
by
Lemma

4.1
. Let us discuss the time needed to fill a single entry.

The unique entry for a leaf node is filled in constant time.
Each entry in a forget node is clearly filled in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.
For an introduce (resp., join) node, we need to iterate over elements of
\mathbb{F}_{t^{\prime}}
(resp.
\mathbb{F}_{t^{\prime}}\times\mathbb{F}_{t^{\prime\prime}}
), which gives us
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
possibilities. For each of them the local computation takes time
n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.

Thus the total running time of the algorithm is
n^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
, as claimed.

Correctness.

To show that the algorithm is correct, we need to show that the property (
\star
) is indeed satisfied.
We will show it in the following two claims.

Claim 4.2.3
.

Let
F
be a maximal induced forest in
G
and let
t\in V(T)
.
Let
(Z,\pi)
be the signature of
F
at
t
.
Then we have
\mathsf{Tab}_{t}[Z,\pi]\geqslant\mathfrak{w}(F\cap V_{t})
.

Proof of Claim .
The proof goes by induction on the depth of
t
.
The base case is when
t
is a leaf.

Leaf node.

As already mentioned, the signature of
F
at
t
is
(\emptyset,\emptyset)
, so by
Lemma

4.1
we have
(\emptyset,\emptyset)\in\mathbb{F}_{t}
. Thus we correctly set
\mathsf{Tab}_{t}[\emptyset,\emptyset]=0
.

Now assume that
t
is not a leaf and the claim holds for all children of
t
.

Introduce node.

Let
t^{\prime}
be the child of
t
and let
v
be the unique vertex in
X_{t}\setminus X_{t^{\prime}}
. Recall that
V_{t}=V_{t^{\prime}}\cup\{v\}
. We consider two cases.

Case 1.
v\notin F
.

This means that
v\notin Z
,
and thus the signature of
F
at
t^{\prime}
is
(Z,\pi)
. In particular, by
Lemma

4.1
, we know that
(Z,\pi)\in\mathbb{F}_{t^{\prime}}
.
We set
\mathsf{Tab}_{t}[Z,\pi]=\mathsf{Tab}_{t^{\prime}}[Z,\pi]\geqslant\mathfrak{w}(F\cap V_{t^{\prime}})=\mathfrak{w}(F\cap V_{t})
, where the “
\geqslant
” inequality follows by the induction hypothesis.

Case 2.
v\in F
.

Let
(Z^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
be the signature of
F
at
t^{\prime}
.
By
Lemma

4.1
we have
(Z^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})\in\mathbb{F}_{t^{\prime}}
.
We note that
Z^{\prime}=Z\setminus\{v\}
.
Let
C
be the component of
F\cap V_{t}
containing
v
,
and let
C_{1},\ldots,C_{p}
be the components of
C\setminus\{v\}
(possibly
p=0
if
C
has just one vertex,
v
).
Note that each
C_{i}
contains exactly one neighbor of
v
.
We note that blocks of
\pi^{\prime}
are:

•

blocks of
\pi
corresponding to components of
F\cap V_{t}
other than
C
; they do not contain neighbors of
v
, and

•

p
blocks, each containing the vertex set of one
C_{i}
; each such component contains one neighbor of
v
.

Thus
\pi^{\prime}
is
v
-good and
\pi^{\prime}\oplus v=\pi
.
Summing up,
(Z^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})=(Z\setminus\{v\},\pi^{\prime})
is one of the options considered when we set the value of
\mathsf{Tab}_{t}[Z,\pi]
in (
2
).
Therefore, by the induction hypothesis, we have

\mathsf{Tab}_{t}[Z,\pi]\geqslant\mathsf{Tab}_{t^{\prime}}[Z,\pi^{\prime}]+\mathfrak{w}(v)\geqslant\mathfrak{w}(F\cap V_{t^{\prime}})+\mathfrak{w}(v)=\mathfrak{w}(F\cap V_{t}),

as claimed.

Forget node.

Let
t^{\prime}
be the child of
t
and let
v
be the unique vertex in
X_{t^{\prime}}\setminus X_{t}
. Recall that
V_{t}=V_{t^{\prime}}
. We consider two cases.

Case 1.
v\notin F
.

Note that in this case the signature of
F
at
t^{\prime}
is
(Z,\pi)
and thus by
Lemma

4.1
this pair is in
\mathbb{F}_{t^{\prime}}
.
Note that we have
(Z,\pi)\in\Sigma\cap\mathbb{F}_{t^{\prime}}
, i.e., in the algorithm we set

\mathsf{Tab}_{t}[Z,\pi]\geqslant\mathsf{Tab}_{t^{\prime}}[Z,\pi]\geqslant\mathfrak{w}(F\cap V_{t^{\prime}})=\mathfrak{w}(F\cap V_{t}),

where the second “
\geqslant
” follows by the induction hypothesis.

Case 2.
v\in F
.

Let
(Z^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
be the signature of
F
at
t^{\prime}
; by
Lemma

4.1
we have
(Z^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})\in\mathbb{F}_{t^{\prime}}
.
Note that we have
Z^{\prime}=Z\cup\{v\}
.
Let
B^{\prime}
be the block of
\pi^{\prime}
containing
v
and denote
B^{\prime}\setminus\{v\}
by
B
.
Since
v\in V_{t}\setminus X_{t}
, the partition
\pi
is obtained from
\pi^{\prime}
by replacing
B^{\prime}
by
B
, or by removing
B^{\prime}
if
B^{\prime}=\{v\}
.
In other words, we have
\pi^{\prime}=\pi_{B}
(with
B=\emptyset
if
B^{\prime}=\{v\}
).
Therefore, in the algorithm we set

\mathsf{Tab}_{t}[Z,\pi]\geqslant\mathsf{Tab}_{t^{\prime}}[Z\cup\{v\},\pi_{B}]\geqslant\mathfrak{w}(F\cap V_{t^{\prime}})=\mathfrak{w}(F\cap V_{t}),

where, again, the second “
\geqslant
” follows by the induction hypothesis.

Join node.

Let
t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime}
be the children of
t
; recall that we have
X_{t}=X_{t^{\prime}}=X_{t^{\prime\prime}}
. Furthermore, it holds that
V_{t}=V_{t^{\prime}}\cup V_{t^{\prime\prime}}
and
V_{t^{\prime}}\cap V_{t^{\prime\prime}}=X_{t}
.

Let
(Z,\pi^{\prime})
(resp.
(Z,\pi^{\prime\prime})
) be the signature of
F
at
t^{\prime}
(resp. at
t^{\prime\prime}
). By
Lemma

4.1
we have
(Z,\pi^{\prime})\in\mathbb{F}_{t^{\prime}}
and
(Z,\pi^{\prime\prime})\in\mathbb{F}_{t^{\prime\prime}}
.
Note that both
\pi^{\prime}
and
\pi^{\prime\prime}
respect the components of
Z
.

We claim that
\pi^{\prime}\otimes\pi^{\prime\prime}=\pi
.
First, let us show that
\mathsf{Merge}(\pi^{\prime},\pi^{\prime\prime})
is a forest.
For contradiction, suppose it contains a cycle
C
.
Recall that
C
has two types of edges: defined by blocks of
\pi^{\prime}
and defined by blocks of
\pi^{\prime\prime}
.
Each edge corresponds to a path in
F
with endpoints in appropriate components of
Z
. By connectivity of each component of
Z
, we observe that
C
defines a cycle in
F
, which is a contradiction as
F
is a forest.

Note that two vertices of
Z
are in the same connected component of
F\cap V_{t}
if and only if one of the following happens: they are in the same connected component of
F\cap V_{t^{\prime}}
, or they are in the same connected component of
F\cap V_{t^{\prime\prime}}
(both possibilities apply if they are in the same component of
Z
).
Thus indeed we have that
\pi^{\prime}\otimes\pi^{\prime\prime}=\pi
.

This means that in the algorithm we set

\displaystyle\mathsf{Tab}_{t}[Z,\pi]\geqslant

\displaystyle\ \mathsf{Tab}_{t^{\prime}}[Z,\pi^{\prime}]+\mathsf{Tab}_{t^{\prime\prime}}[Z,\pi^{\prime\prime}]-\mathfrak{w}(Z)\geqslant\ \mathfrak{w}(F\cap V_{t^{\prime}})+\mathfrak{w}(F\cap V_{t^{\prime\prime}})-\mathfrak{w}(Z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathfrak{w}(F\cap V_{t})+\mathfrak{w}(F\cap X_{t})-\mathfrak{w}(Z)=\mathfrak{w}(F\cap V_{t}),

where the second “
\geqslant
” follows by the induction hypothesis.
This completes the proof.

\triangleleft

Claim 4.2.4
.

Let
t\in V(T)
and let
(Z,\pi)\in\mathbb{F}_{t}
such that
\mathsf{Tab}_{t}[Z,\pi]\neq-\infty
.
Then there exists an induced forest
F
in
V_{t}
with signature
(Z,\pi)
at
t
satisfying
\mathfrak{w}(F)\geqslant\mathsf{Tab}_{t}[Z,\pi]
.

Proof of Claim .
The proof again goes by induction on the depth of
t
.
The base case is that
t
is a leaf node.

Leaf node.

If
t
is a leaf node, then the only choice for
(Z,\pi)
is
(\emptyset,\emptyset)
, and we set
\mathsf{Tab}_{t}[\emptyset,\emptyset]=0
.
Note that
F=\emptyset
satisfies the desired properties.

So from now on we assume that
t
is not a leaf and the claim holds for all children of
t
. Again, the argument depends on the type of
t
.

Introduce node.

Let
t
be an introduce node,
t^{\prime}
be its child in
T
, and let
v
be the unique vertex in
X_{t}\setminus X_{t^{\prime}}
.
Since
\mathsf{Tab}_{t}[Z,\pi]\neq-\infty
, its value was set either in Case 1 or in Case 2 of the algorithm.
Consider these cases separately.

Case 1.
This means that
\mathsf{Tab}_{t}[Z,\pi]=\mathsf{Tab}_{t^{\prime}}[Z,\pi]
.
By the induction hypothesis there is an induced forest
F\subseteq V_{t^{\prime}}
with signature
(Z,\pi)
at
t^{\prime}
with
\mathfrak{w}(F)\geqslant\mathsf{Tab}_{t^{\prime}}[Z,\pi]
.
Notice that the signature of
F
at
t
is
(Z,\pi)
.
Moreover,
F
satisfies
\mathfrak{w}(F)\geqslant\mathsf{Tab}_{t^{\prime}}[Z,\pi]=\mathsf{Tab}_{t}[Z,\pi]
, as claimed.

Case 2.
This means that
\mathsf{Tab}_{t}[Z,\pi]=\mathsf{Tab}_{t^{\prime}}[Z\setminus\{v\},\pi^{\prime}]+\mathfrak{w}(v)
for some
(Z\setminus\{v\},\pi^{\prime})\in\mathbb{F}_{t^{\prime}}
, such that
\pi^{\prime}
is
v
-good and
\pi^{\prime}\oplus v=\pi
, as set in (
2
).
As
\mathsf{Tab}_{t}[Z,\pi]\neq-\infty
, we also have
\mathsf{Tab}_{t^{\prime}}[Z\setminus\{v\},\pi^{\prime}]\neq-\infty
, so by the induction hypothesis there exists an induced forest
F^{\prime}\subseteq V_{t^{\prime}}
with signature
(Z\setminus\{v\},\pi^{\prime})
at
t^{\prime}
, satisfying
\mathfrak{w}(F^{\prime})\geqslant\mathsf{Tab}_{t^{\prime}}[Z\setminus\{v\},\pi^{\prime}]
.

We aim to show that
F\coloneqq F^{\prime}\cup\{v\}
satisfies the statement of the claim.
First we need to argue that
F
is an induced forest.
For contradiction suppose that
F
contains a cycle
C
.
Since
F^{\prime}
is acyclic, we observe that
v
belongs to
C
and
C-v
is contained in one component of
F^{\prime}
.
As the neighborhood of
v
in
V_{t^{\prime}}
is contained in
X_{t^{\prime}}
, we observe that the neighbors of
v
on
C
, say
u
and
w
, are in
Z\setminus\{v\}
.
Consequently,
u,w
are in one block of
\pi^{\prime}
and so
\pi^{\prime}
is not
v
-good, a contradiction.

Now let us argue that the signature of
F
at
t
is
(Z,\pi)
.
Clearly
F\cap V_{t}=F\cap\left(V_{t^{\prime}}\cup\{v\}\right)=(Z\setminus\{v\})\cup\{v\}=Z
.
On the other hand, it is straightforward to verify that the partition of
Z
corresponding to components of
F
is exactly
\pi^{\prime}\oplus v=\pi
.
Finally, note that
\mathfrak{w}(F)=\mathfrak{w}(F^{\prime})+\mathfrak{w}(v)\geqslant\mathsf{Tab}_{t^{\prime}}[Z\setminus\{v\},\pi^{\prime}]+\mathfrak{w}(v)=\mathsf{Tab}_{t}[Z,\pi]
.

Forget node.

Let
t^{\prime}
be the child of
t
and let
v
be the unique vertex in
X_{t^{\prime}}\setminus X_{t}
.
As
\mathsf{Tab}_{t}[Z,\pi]\neq-\infty
, its value was set to
\mathsf{Tab}_{t^{\prime}}[Z^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}]
for some
(Z^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})\in\Sigma\cap\mathbb{F}_{t^{\prime}}
, as in (
3
).

Let
F
be an induced forest in
V_{t^{\prime}}
with signature
(Z^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
at
t^{\prime}
, such that
\mathfrak{w}(F)\geqslant\mathsf{Tab}_{t^{\prime}}[Z^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}]
.
Consider two cases.

Case 1.
v\notin F
.
Then by the definition of
\Sigma
we observe that
Z^{\prime}=Z
and
\pi^{\prime}=\pi
.
The signature of
F
at
t
is
(Z,\pi)
.

Case 2.
v\in F
.
Then by the definition of
\Sigma
we have that
Z^{\prime}=Z\cup\{v\}
and
\pi^{\prime}=\pi_{B}
for some block
B
of
\pi
or
\pi^{\prime}=\pi_{\emptyset}
.
Note that the signature of
F
at
t
is
(Z,\pi)
.

In both cases we have
\mathfrak{w}(F)\geqslant\mathsf{Tab}_{t^{\prime}}[Z^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}]=\mathsf{Tab}_{t}[Z,\pi]
, so
F
satisfies the statement of the claim.

Join node.

Let
t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime}
be the children of
t
; recall that we have
X_{t}=X_{t^{\prime}}=X_{t^{\prime\prime}}
.
Since
\mathsf{Tab}_{t}[Z,\pi]\neq-\infty
, we have
\mathsf{Tab}_{t}[Z,\pi]=(\mathsf{Tab}_{t^{\prime}}[Z,\pi^{\prime}]+\mathsf{Tab}_{t^{\prime\prime}}[Z,\pi^{\prime\prime}])-\mathfrak{w}(Z)

for some
(Z,\pi^{\prime})\in\mathbb{F}_{t^{\prime}}
and
(Z,\pi^{\prime\prime})\in\mathbb{F}_{t^{\prime\prime}}
such that
\pi^{\prime}\otimes\pi^{\prime\prime}=\pi
,
as set in (
4
).

Let
F^{\prime}
(resp.
F^{\prime\prime}
) be an induced forest in
V_{t^{\prime}}
(resp.
V_{t^{\prime\prime}}
) with signature
(Z,\pi^{\prime})
at
t^{\prime}
(resp.
(Z,\pi^{\prime\prime})
at
t^{\prime\prime}
)
such that
\mathfrak{w}(F^{\prime})\geqslant\mathsf{Tab}_{t^{\prime}}[Z,\pi^{\prime}]
(resp.
\mathfrak{w}(F^{\prime\prime})\geqslant\mathsf{Tab}_{t^{\prime\prime}}[Z,\pi^{\prime\prime}]
).

We claim that
F\coloneqq F^{\prime}\cup F^{\prime\prime}
is an induced forest in
V_{t}
.
For contradiction, suppose that
F
contains a cycle.
Note that such a cycle cannot be contained in
Z
as we know that
Z
induces a forest (since the entries of
\mathsf{Tab}
corresponding to
Z
have finite values).
So in particular
F
contains a cycle
C
whose intersection with every component of
Z
is a subpath of
C
.
Let
C^{\prime}
be the multigraph obtained from
C
by (1) contracting every edge with both endvertices in
Z
, and then (2) substituting every maximal path with all interior vertices outside
Z
by a single edge.
Note that this step might have created multiple edges, but no loops, since both
F^{\prime}
and
F^{\prime\prime}
are induced forests and
Z
does not contain cycles.
Thus
C^{\prime}
is a cycle whose every vertex corresponds to a distinct component of
Z
.
Every edge of
C^{\prime}
indicates that either
F^{\prime}
or
F^{\prime\prime}
contains a path with endpoints in particular components of
Z
.
Therefore
C^{\prime}
is a sub(multi)graph of
\textsf{Merge}(\pi^{\prime},\pi^{\prime\prime})
