, so in particular
\textsf{Merge}(\pi^{\prime},\pi^{\prime\prime})
is not a simple forest.
Consequently,
\pi^{\prime}\otimes\pi^{\prime\prime}
is not defined, so it cannot be equal to
\pi
.

Clearly
F\cap X_{t}=Z
. Furthermore, the partition of
Z
corresponding to components of
F
is precisely
\pi=\pi^{\prime}\otimes\pi^{\prime\prime}
,
so
(Z,\pi)
is the signature of
F
at
t
.

Finally we notice that
\mathfrak{w}(F)=\mathfrak{w}(F^{\prime})+\mathfrak{w}(F^{\prime\prime})-\mathfrak{w}(F^{\prime}\cap F^{\prime\prime})=\mathfrak{w}(F^{\prime})+\mathfrak{w}(F^{\prime\prime})-\mathfrak{w}(Z)\geqslant\mathsf{Tab}_{t^{\prime}}[Z,\pi^{\prime}]+\mathsf{Tab}_{t^{\prime\prime}}[Z,\pi^{\prime\prime}]-\mathfrak{w}(Z)=\mathsf{Tab}_{t}[Z,\pi]
.
This completes the proof of claim.

\triangleleft

Now let us argue that
4.2.3
and
4.2.4
are sufficient to prove the correctness of our algorithm.
Let
F
be an optimal solution. We can safely assume that
F
is a maximal induced forest.
Recall that its signature at
\mathsf{root}(T)
is
(\emptyset,\emptyset)
.
By
4.2.3
we have that
\mathsf{Tab}_{\mathsf{root}(T)}[\emptyset,\emptyset]\geqslant\mathfrak{w}(F)
.
Now suppose for a contradiction that
\mathsf{Tab}_{\mathsf{root}(T)}[\emptyset,\emptyset]>\mathfrak{w}(F)
.
Thus, by
4.2.4
, there is an induced forest
F^{*}
in
G
with
\mathfrak{w}(F^{*})\geqslant\mathsf{Tab}_{\mathsf{root}(T)}[\emptyset,\emptyset]>\mathfrak{w}(F)
. However, this contradicts the optimality of
F
.
Therefore,
\mathsf{Tab}_{\mathsf{root}(T)}[\emptyset,\emptyset]
is indeed the weight of an optimal solution.
This completes the proof of
Lemma

4.2
and thus of
Theorem

2.1
.

5
Packing independent induced subgraphs

Recall that given a graph
G
and a family
\mathcal{H}=\{H_{j}\}_{j\in J}
of connected subgraphs of
G
, we denote by
G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}]
the graph with vertex set
J
, in which two distinct vertices
i,j\in J
are adjacent if and only if
H_{i}
and
H_{j}
either have a vertex in common or there is an edge in
G
connecting them.
The starting point of our investigations will be the following result of Dallard et al.
[
32
]
.

Lemma 5.1

(Dallard et al.
[
32
]
)
.

Let
G
be a graph, let
\mathcal{T}=(T,\{X_{t}\}_{t\in V(T)})
be a tree decomposition of
G
, and let
\mathcal{H}=\{H_{j}\}_{j\in J}
be a finite family of connected non-null subgraphs of
G
.
Then
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}=\big(T,\{X^{\prime}_{t}\}_{t\in V(T)}\big)
with
X^{\prime}_{t}=\{{j\in J}\colon V(H_{j})\cap X_{t}\neq\emptyset\}
for all
t\in V(T)
is a tree decomposition of
G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}]
such that
\alpha(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})\leqslant\alpha(\mathcal{T})
.

Corollary 5.2

(Dallard et al.
[
32
]
)
.

Let
G
be a graph and let
\mathcal{H}=\{H_{j}\}_{j\in J}
be a finite family of connected non-null subgraphs of
G
.
Then
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}])\leqslant\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G)
.

5.1
Tree-independence number and induced matching treewidth of
G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}]

In this section we prove results similar to
Corollary

5.2
, but with a focus on induced matching treewidth.
Note that while in the statement of
Corollary

5.2
, the family
\mathcal{H}=\{H_{j}\}_{j\in J}
of subgraphs of
G
may be a multiset, in the first part of the next lemma we restrict ourselves to a
set
of subgraphs, with no repetitions allowed.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
G
be a graph and
\mathcal{H}=\{H_{j}\}_{j\in J}
be a set of connected non-null subgraphs of
G
.
Then,
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}])\leqslant\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G)
.
Furthermore, if all graphs in
\mathcal{H}
have at least two vertices, then
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}])\leqslant\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G)
, even if
\mathcal{H}
is a multiset.

Proof.

Let
k=\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G)
and consider a tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}=(T,\{X_{t}\}_{t\in V(T)})
of the graph
G
such that
\mu_{G}(\mathcal{T})=k
.
We want to construct a tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}=(T,\{X^{\prime}_{t}\}_{t\in V(T)})
for
G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}]
such that
\mu_{G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}]}(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})\leqslant k
.
Note that
V(G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}])=J
.
For each
t\in V(T)
, let
X^{\prime}_{t}=\{{j\in J}\colon V(H_{j})\cap X_{t}\neq\emptyset\}
.
By
Lemma

5.1
,
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
is a tree decomposition of the graph
G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}]
.
We now show that
\mu_{G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}]}(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})\leqslant k
.
This will establish
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}])\leqslant\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G)
.
Consider a bag of
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
, say
X^{\prime}_{t}
for some
t\in V(T)
, and suppose for a contradiction that there is an induced matching
M
in the graph
G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}]
with cardinality
k+1
such that each edge of
M
has an endpoint in
X^{\prime}_{t}
.
Then, there exists a set
I\subseteq X_{t}^{\prime}
with
|I|=k+1
such that
I
contains precisely one endpoint of each edge of
M
.
Consider an arbitrary edge
\{i,i^{\prime}\}
of
M
such that
i\in I
.
Since
i\in X^{\prime}_{t}
, there exists a vertex
v_{i}\in X_{t}\cap V(H_{i})
.
Since
\{i,i^{\prime}\}
is an edge in
G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}]
, the union of the graphs
H_{i}
and
H_{i^{\prime}}
is a connected subgraph of
G
containing at least two vertices; in particular, no vertex in
H_{i}\cup H_{i^{\prime}}
is isolated.
Let
v_{i^{\prime}}
be a neighbor of
v_{i}
in
H_{i}\cup H_{i^{\prime}}
.
Since
M
is an induced matching in
G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}]
, note that if
\{i,i^{\prime}\},\{j,j^{\prime}\}\in M
with
i,j\in I
and
i\neq j
, then there is no edge in
G
connecting a vertex of
H_{i}\cup H_{i^{\prime}}
to a vertex of
H_{j}\cup H_{j^{\prime}}
.
Since for each
i\in I
,
v_{i}v_{i^{\prime}}
is an edge of
H_{i}\cup H_{i^{\prime}}
, we conclude that
\{v_{i}v_{i^{\prime}}\colon{i\in I}\}
is an induced matching in
G
.
Furthermore, each of the edges in this matching has an endpoint in
X_{t}
.
This implies that
\mu_{G}(\mathcal{T})>k
, a contradiction.

Assume now that each graph in
\mathcal{H}
has at least two vertices, but repetitions of subgraphs in
\mathcal{H}
are allowed.
We now show that
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}])\leqslant\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G)
.
Consider the same tree decomposition of
G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}]
as before.
In this case, we can show, using a similar argument as above, that
\alpha_{G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}]}(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})\leqslant k
, which will establish
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}])\leqslant\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G)
.
Consider a bag of
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
, say
X^{\prime}_{t}
for some
t\in V(T)
, and suppose for a contradiction that there is an independent set
I
in the graph
G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}]
with cardinality
k+1
such that
I\subseteq X^{\prime}_{t}
.
Consider an arbitrary
i\in I
.
Since
i\in X^{\prime}_{t}
, there exists a vertex
v_{i}\in X_{t}\cap V(H_{i})
.
Since the graph
H_{i}
is connected and has at least two vertices, there exists a vertex
v_{i^{\prime}}
that is adjacent to
v_{i}
in
H_{i}
.
Since for each
i\in I
, the edge
v_{i}v_{i^{\prime}}
is an edge of
H_{i}
, the fact that
I
is an independent set in
G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}]
and the definition of the graph
G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}]
imply that
\{v_{i}{v_{i^{\prime}}\colon i\in I}\}
is an induced matching in
G
.
Furthermore, each of the edges in this matching has an endpoint in
X_{t}
.
This implies that
\mu_{G}(\mathcal{T})>k
, a contradiction.
∎

Remark 5.4
.

In the first part of
Lemma

5.3
, the assumption that
\mathcal{H}
is a set is necessary.
To see this, fix a positive integer
n
, let
G
be the complete bipartite graph
K_{n,n}
, and let
\mathcal{H}
be the multiset of connected subgraphs of
G
consisting of two copies of each one-vertex induced subgraph of
G
.
Then
G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}]
is the graph obtained from two disjoint copies of the graph
nK_{2}
by adding all possible edges between them, where
nK_{2}
denotes the disjoint union of
n
copies of
K_{2}
.
Clearly,
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G)=1
, but, as we will show in
Section

7
(see
Proposition

7.10
),
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}])\geqslant n
.

The same construction also shows that the assumption that all graphs in
\mathcal{H}
have at least two vertices is necessary in the second part of
Lemma

5.3
.

As the proof of
Lemma

5.3
is algorithmic, we obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 5.5
.

Given a graph
G
, a family
\mathcal{H}
of connected non-null subgraphs of
G
, and a tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}=(T,\{X_{t}\}_{t\in V(T)})
of
G
, in time polynomial in
|V(G)|,|\mathcal{H}|,
and
|V(T)|
we can compute a tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
of
G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}]
such that
\mu_{G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}]}(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})\leqslant\mu_{G}(\mathcal{T})
if
\mathcal{H}
is a set, and
\alpha_{G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}]}(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})\leqslant\mu_{G}(\mathcal{T})
if all graphs in
\mathcal{H}
have at least two vertices.

5.1.1
Algorithmic consequences

Let us now explain how
Lemma

5.3
can be used algorithmically.
Given a graph
G
and a family
\mathcal{H}=\{H_{j}\}_{j\in J}
of subgraphs of
G
, a subfamily
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{H}
is said to be an
independent
\mathcal{H}
-packing
in
G
if every two graphs in
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
are vertex-disjoint and there is no edge in
G
between them. In other words,
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
corresponds to an independent set in the graph
G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}]
.
Assume now that the subgraphs in
\mathcal{H}
are equipped with a weight function
\mathfrak{w}\colon J\to\mathbb{Q}_{+}
assigning weight
\mathfrak{w}_{j}
to each subgraph
H_{j}
.
For any set
J^{\prime}\subseteq J
, we define the
weight
of the family
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=\{H_{j}\}_{j\in J^{\prime}}
as the sum
\sum_{j\in J^{\prime}}\mathfrak{w}_{j}
.
In particular, the weight of any independent
\mathcal{H}
-packing is well-defined.

Given a graph
G
, a finite family
\mathcal{H}=\{H_{j}\}_{j\in J}
of connected non-null subgraphs of
G
, and a weight function
\mathfrak{w}\colon J\to\mathbb{Q}_{+}
on the subgraphs in
\mathcal{H}
, the
Max Weight Independent Packing
problem asks to find an independent
\mathcal{H}
-packing in
G
of maximum weight.
A natural special case of the problem is when for each
j\in J
, the value of
\mathfrak{w}_{j}
is equal to the number of vertices of
H_{j}
; we call this restriction
Max Independent Packing

[
25
]
.

It is straightforward to verify that
Max Weight Independent Packing
for
G
and
\mathcal{H}
is equivalent to
MWIS
in
G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}]
, where the weights of vertices of
G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}]
correspond to the weights of subgraphs in
\mathcal{H}
.

Theorem 5.6
.

Given a graph
G
and a finite family
\mathcal{H}=\{H_{j}\}_{j\in J}
of connected non-null subgraphs of
G
, and a weight function
\mathfrak{w}\colon J\to\mathbb{Q}_{+}
, the
Max Weight Independent Packing
problem can be solved in time polynomial in
|V(G)|
and
|\mathcal{H}|
for graphs of bounded induced matching treewidth.

Sketch of proof.

The proof follows the reasoning of Dallard et al.
[
32
]
for tree-independence number, so let us just sketch it.

Let
G
and
\mathcal{H}
be as in the statement and let
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G)\leqslant k
, where
k
is a constant.
First we preprocess the input family
\mathcal{H}=\{H_{j}\}_{j\in J}
by keeping at most one copy of each subgraph of
G
.
Indeed, if
H_{i}=H_{j}
for two distinct
i,j\in J
such that
\mathfrak{w}(i)\leqslant\mathfrak{w}(j)
, then not both
H_{i}
and
H_{j}
can appear in an independent
\mathcal{H}
-packing, and discarding
H_{i}
from the family
\mathcal{H}
results in an equivalent instance.
After this preprocessing step, the resulting family
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=\{H_{j}\}_{j\in J^{\prime}}
is a set..
Thus, by
Lemma

5.3
, we have
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}^{\prime}])\leqslant\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G)\leqslant k
.
Finally, we compute
G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}^{\prime}]
and solve the
MWIS
problem on
G^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}^{\prime}]
(and the restriction of
\mathfrak{w}
to
J^{\prime}
) using
Theorem

1.1
.
Since
k
is a constant, the overall running time is polynomial in
|V(G)|
and
|\mathcal{H}|
.
∎

A class
\mathcal{C}
of graphs is
weakly hyperfinite
if for every
\epsilon>0
there is
c(\epsilon)\in\mathbb{N}

such that in every graph
G\in\mathcal{C}
there is a subset
X\subseteq V(G)
of at least
(1-\epsilon)|V(G)|
vertices such that every connected component of
G-X
has at most
c(\epsilon)
vertices
[
56
, Section 16.2]
.
It turns out that every class that is closed under vertex and edge deletions and admits sublinear balanced separators is weakly hyperfinite. Many well-known classes of sparse graphs are weakly hyperfinite, e.g., graphs of bounded treewidth, planar graphs and, more generally, graphs of bounded genus. In fact, all proper minor-closed classes are weakly hyperfinite.

Theorem 5.7
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a weakly hyperfinite class of graphs closed under vertex deletion and disjoint union operations.
Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\epsilon>0
be fixed.
Given a graph
G
with induced matching treewidth at most
k
,
in polynomial time we can find a set
F\subseteq V(G)
such that:

1.

G[F]\in\mathcal{C}
,

2.

the size of
F
is at least
(1-\epsilon)\textsf{OPT}
, where
OPT
is the size of a largest set satisfying the first condition.

Sketch of proof.

We again only provide a sketch of proof and refer the reader to Gartland et al. for further details
[
38
]
.
Let
F^{*}
be an optimal solution. Since
\mathcal{C}
is weakly hyperfinite, there exists a set
F^{\prime}\subseteq F^{*}
such that
|F^{\prime}|\geqslant(1-\epsilon)|F^{*}|
and every component of
F^{\prime}
has at most
c(\epsilon)
vertices.
As
\mathcal{C}
is closed under vertex deletion, we observe that
F^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
.
Let
\mathcal{H}
be the family of induced subgraphs of
G
of size at most
c(\epsilon)
that belong to
\mathcal{C}
.
Note that
\mathcal{H}
can be enumerated in time polynomial in
V(G)
as
c(\epsilon)
is a constant.

We call the algorithm from
Theorem

5.6
for
G,\mathcal{H}
, and the weight function defined as the number of vertices for each of
\mathcal{H}
. Let
F
be an optimal solution for this instance.
As
\mathcal{C}
is closed under disjoint union, we observe that
F\in\mathcal{C}
. Furthermore, we have
|F|\geqslant|F^{\prime}|\geqslant(1-\epsilon)|F^{*}|
.
∎

As bounded-treewidth graphs form a weakly hyperfinite class, we immediately obtain the following corollary.

See
2.4

5.2
Tree-independence number and induced matching treewidth of graph powers

Now let us turn our attention to powers of graphs of bounded induced matching treewidth.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
G
be a graph, and
k
and
d
be positive integers.
For
v\in V(G)
, let
H_{v}
be the subgraph of
G
induced by the vertices at distance at most
d
from
v
, and let
\mathcal{H}=\{H_{v}\}_{v\in V(G)}
.
Then, the graphs
G^{k+2d}
and
(G^{k})^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}]
are isomorphic.

Proof.

Let us first observe that for every vertex
v\in V(G)
, the graph
H_{v}
is a connected subgraph of
G^{k}
.
Hence, the graph
(G^{k})^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}]
is well-defined.

Note that the graphs
G^{k+2d}
and
(G^{k})^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}]
both have vertex sets of the same size.
Moreover, there is a bijection that maps each
v\in V(G^{k+2d})
to the vertex corresponding to
H_{v}
in
(G^{k})^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}]
. For simplicity of notation, we denote both vertex sets by
V(G)
.

We now show that for any
u,v\in V(G)
,
uv\in E(G^{k+2d})
if and only if
uv\in E((G^{k})^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}])
, which will allow us to conclude that the graphs
G^{k+2d}
and
(G^{k})^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}]
are isomorphic.

Suppose first that
u
and
v
are adjacent in
(G^{k})^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}]
.
Then the graphs
H_{u}
and
H_{v}
either have a vertex in common, or they are vertex-disjoint but there is an edge between them in
G^{k}
.
If
H_{u}
and
H_{v}
have a vertex in common, say
x\in V(H_{u})\cap V(H_{v})
, then
\textsf{dist}_{G}(u,v)\leqslant\textsf{dist}_{G}(u,x)+\textsf{dist}_{G}(x,v)\leqslant 2d\leqslant k+2d
, and hence
u
and
v
are adjacent in
G^{k+2d}
.
If
H_{u}
and
H_{v}
are vertex disjoint but there exist vertices
x\in V(H_{u})
and
y\in V(H_{v})
such that
xy\in E(G^{k})
, then
\textsf{dist}_{G}(u,v)\leqslant\textsf{dist}_{G}(u,x)+\textsf{dist}_{G}(x,y)+\textsf{dist}_{G}(y,v)\leqslant d+k+d=k+2d
, and hence
u
and
v
are adjacent in
G^{k+2d}
.

Suppose now that
u
and
v
are adjacent in
G^{k+2d}
.
Then
\textsf{dist}_{G}(u,v)\leqslant k+2d
.
We want to show that the graphs
H_{u}
and
H_{v}
either have a vertex in common, or they are vertex-disjoint but there is an edge between them in
G^{k}
.
If
u
and
v
are at distance at most
2d
in
G
, then there exists a vertex
x\in{V(G)}
that is at distance in
G
at most
d
from each of
u
and
v
, and hence the graphs
H_{u}
and
H_{v}
have a vertex in common.
We may thus assume that
\textsf{dist}_{G}(u,v)\geqslant 2d+1
or, equivalently, that the graphs
H_{u}
and
H_{v}
are vertex-disjoint.
Since
2d+1\leqslant\textsf{dist}_{G}(u,v)\leqslant k+2d
, there exists a
u
-
v
-path
P=(u=u_{0},u_{1},\ldots,u_{q}=v)
in
G
such that
2d+1\leqslant q\leqslant k+2d
.
This implies that
1\leqslant\textsf{dist}_{G}(u_{d},u_{q-d})\leqslant k
and hence the vertices
u_{d}
and
u_{q-d}
are adjacent in
G^{k}
.
Since
u_{d}
is at distance at most
d
from
u
, it belongs to
H_{u}
.
Similarly,
u_{q-d}
belongs to
H_{v}
.
Thus,
u_{d}u_{q-d}\in E(G^{k})
, and hence is an edge between the subgraphs
H_{u}
and
H_{v}
.
This shows that
u
and
v
are adjacent in
(G^{k})^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}]
.
∎

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
G
be a graph with at least one edge and
r
a positive integer.
Then

\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G^{r+2})\leqslant\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G^{r+2})\leqslant\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G^{r})\leqslant\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G^{r})\,.

Proof.

Since
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(H)\leqslant\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(H)
for every graph
H
, it suffices to show that
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G^{r+2})\leqslant\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G^{r})
.
Suppose first that
G
is connected.
By
Lemma

5.8
(applied with
k=r
and
d=1
), the graph
G^{r+2}
is isomorphic to the graph
(G^{r})^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}]
, where
\mathcal{H}=\{H_{v}\}_{v\in{V(G)}}
and
H_{v}
is the subgraph of
G
induced by the vertices at distance at most
1
from
v
.
Therefore,
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G^{r+2})=\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu((G^{r})^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}])
.
By definition, each subgraph in
\mathcal{H}
is connected.
Furthermore, since
G
is a connected graph with at least two vertices, every vertex
v\in V(G)
has a neighbor and hence
H_{v}
has at least two vertices.
Hence, by
Lemma

5.3
,
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha((G^{r})^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}])\leqslant\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G^{r})
, and we conclude
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G^{r+2})\leqslant\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G^{r})
.

Now, let us consider the general case.
Let
\ell=\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G^{r})
.
Since
G
has an edge, so does
G^{r}
and consequently
\ell\geqslant 1
.
By the first case, each nontrivial connected component
C
of
G
satisfies
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(C^{r+2})\leqslant\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(C^{r})\leqslant\ell
.
Since
\ell\geqslant 1
, each component
C
of
G
with only one vertex satisfies
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(C^{r+2})\leqslant\ell
.
By combining tree decompositons with independence number at most
\ell
of each component of
G^{r+2}
, we obtain a tree decomposition of
G^{r+2}
with independence number at most
\ell
.
Therefore,
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G^{r+2})\leqslant\ell
.
∎

Lemma

5.9
is a significant generalization of a result of Duchet
[
34
]
, who proved that for every positive integer
r
, if
G^{r}
is chordal, then so is
G^{r+2}
.
This corresponds to the case when
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G^{r})\leqslant 1
.

Corollary 5.10
.

For any graph
G
and positive integer
r
, we have
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G^{r+2})\leqslant\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G^{r})

and

\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G^{r+2})\leqslant\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G^{r})
.

Proof.

If
G
has no vertices, then
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G^{r})=\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G^{r+2})=\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G^{r})=\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G^{r+2})=0
and the statement holds.
Assume now that
G
is non-null.
If
G
is edgeless, then so are
G^{r}
and
G^{r+2}
; hence
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G^{r})=\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G^{r+2})=1
and

\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G^{r})=\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G^{r+2})=0
and the statement holds.
Finally, assume that
G
has at least one edge.
Then, by
Lemma

5.9
we have
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G^{r+2})\leqslant\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G^{r+2})\leqslant\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G^{r})\leqslant\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G^{r})
, which implies both stated inequalities.
∎

Corollary 5.11
.

Let
G
be a graph and
r
be a positive odd integer. Then
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G^{r})\leqslant\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G)
and
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G^{r})\leqslant\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G)
.

Corollary

5.11
generalizes a result due to Balakrishnan and Paulraja
[
9
]
stating that the class of chordal graphs is closed under taking odd powers.

Lemma

5.9
and a straightforward induction on
r
also implies the following.

Corollary 5.12
.

Let
G
be a graph and
r\geqslant 3
an odd integer. Then
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G^{r})\leqslant\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G)
.

Furthermore, as all our proofs are algorithmic, we obtain the following.

Corollary 5.13
.

For every odd integer
r\geqslant 3
, there exists an algorithm that takes as input a graph
G
and a tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}=(T,\{X_{t}\}_{t\in V(T)})
of
G
, and computes in time polynomial in
|V(G)|

the graph
G^{r}
and a tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}=(T,\{X^{\prime}_{t}\}_{t\in V(T)})
of
G^{r}
such that
\alpha_{G^{r}}(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})\leqslant\mu_{G}(\mathcal{T})
.

5.2.1
Algorithmic consequences

Let
d
be a positive integer, let
G
be a graph, let
\mathcal{H}=\{H_{j}\}_{j\in J}
be a finite family of connected non-null subgraphs of
G
, and let
J^{\prime}\subseteq J
.
A
distance-
d

\mathcal{H}
-packing
in
G
is a subfamily
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=\{H_{j}\}_{j\in J^{\prime}}
of subgraphs from
\mathcal{H}
that are at pairwise distance at least
d
in
G
.

Observation 5.14
.

Let
d
be a positive integer, let
G
be a graph, let
\mathcal{H}=\{H_{j}\}_{j\in J}
be a finite family of connected non-null subgraphs of
G
, and let
J^{\prime}\subseteq J
.
Then, the corresponding subfamily
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=\{H_{i}\}_{j\in J^{\prime}}
is a distance-
d

\mathcal{H}
-packing in
G
if and only if
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
is an independent
\mathcal{H}
-packing in the graph
G^{d-1}
.

Proof.

Consider two distinct elements
i,j\in J^{\prime}
.
It suffices to show that
\textsf{dist}_{G}(V(H_{i}),V(H_{j}))\geqslant d
if and only if
\textsf{dist}_{G^{d-1}}(V(H_{i}),V(H_{j}))\geqslant 2
, or, equivalently, that
\textsf{dist}_{G}(V(H_{i}),V(H_{j}))\leqslant d-1
if and only if
\textsf{dist}_{G^{d-1}}(V(H_{i}),V(H_{j}))\leqslant 1
.

Suppose first that
\textsf{dist}_{G}(V(H_{i}),V(H_{j}))\leqslant d-1
and let
P=(v_{0},\ldots,v_{r})
be a path in
G
from a vertex in
H_{i}
to a vertex in
H_{j}
such that
r\leqslant d-1
.
If
r=0
then
\textsf{dist}_{G^{d-1}}(V(H_{i}),V(H_{j}))=0
.
If
r>0
, then in
G^{d-1}
, vertices
v_{0}
and
v_{r}
are adjacent, and hence
\textsf{dist}_{G^{d-1}}(V(H_{i}),V(H_{j}))\leqslant 1
.

Suppose now that
\textsf{dist}_{G^{d-1}}(V(H_{i}),V(H_{j}))\leqslant 1
.
If this distance is
0
, then the graphs
H_{i}
and
H_{j}
have a vertex in common and hence
\textsf{dist}_{G}(V(H_{i}),V(H_{j}))=0\leqslant d-1
.
If this distance is
1
, then there is a path
P
in
G
of length at most
d-1
from a vertex in
H_{i}
to a vertex in
H_{j}
.
Thus,
\textsf{dist}_{G}(V(H_{i}),V(H_{j}))\leqslant d-1
.
∎

For an integer
d
, the input to
Max Weight Distance-
d
Packing
is a graph
G
, a finite family
\mathcal{H}=\{H_{j}\}_{j\in J}
of connected non-null subgraphs of
G
, and a weight function
\mathfrak{w}\colon J\to\mathbb{Q}_{+}
on the subgraphs in
\mathcal{H}
.
The problem asks for a maximum-weight distance-
d

\mathcal{H}
-packing in
G
.
By
5.14
, for every
J^{\prime}\subseteq J
, the corresponding subfamily
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=\{H_{j}\}_{j\in J^{\prime}}
is a distance-
d

\mathcal{H}
-packing in
G
if and only if
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
is an independent
\mathcal{H}
-packing in the graph
G^{d-1}
.

Theorem 5.15
.

For every positive integer
k
and even positive integer
d
, given a graph
G
of induced matching treewidth at most
k
, a finite family
\mathcal{H}=\{H_{j}\}_{j\in J}
of connected non-null subgraphs of
G
, and a weight function
\mathfrak{w}\colon J\to\mathbb{Q}_{+}
on the subgraphs in
\mathcal{H}
, the
Max Weight Distance-
d
Packing
problem is solvable in time polynomial in
|V(G)|
and
|\mathcal{H}|
.

Proof.

If
d=2
, then the
Max Weight Distance-
d
Packing
problem coincides with the
Max Weight Independent Packing
problem and the conclusion follows from
Theorem

5.6
.
So we may assume that
d\geqslant 4
.

We compute the graphs
G^{d-1}
and
(G^{d-1})^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}]
; note that the latter graph is well-defined, since every graph in
\mathcal{H}
is a connected non-null subgraph of
G^{d-1}
.
By
Corollary

5.12
,
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G^{d-1})\leqslant\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G)
, and by
Corollary

5.2
,
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha((G^{d-1})^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}])\leqslant\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G^{d-1})
.
Therefore,
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu((G^{d-1})^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}])\leqslant\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha((G^{d-1})^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}])\leqslant\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G)=k
.
By
5.14
, a subfamily
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{H}
is a distance-
d

\mathcal{H}
-packing in
G
if and only if
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
is an independent
\mathcal{H}
-packing in the graph
G^{d-1}
, which is also equivalent to
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
being an independent set in the graph
(G^{d-1})^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}]
.
Finally, using the fact that
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu((G^{d-1})^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}])\leqslant k
, we call
Theorem

1.1
to solve the instance
((G^{d-1})^{\circ}[\mathcal{H}],\mathfrak{w})
of
MWIS
.
The overall running time is polynomial in
|V(G)|
and
|\mathcal{H}|
, as
k
is a constant.
∎

Note that in the last step of the above proof, instead of
Theorem

1.1
we could have also used an algorithm for
MWIS
for graphs with bounded
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha
(see
[
32
]
).

We remark that unless
\textsf{P}=\textsf{NP}
, the result of
Theorem

5.15
cannot be generalized to odd values of
d\geqslant 3
, since in this case the distance-
d
variant of
Max Independent Set
is
NP
-hard for chordal graphs (see
[
35
]
), which have induced matching treewidth (and even tree-independence number) at most one.
Similarly, a result analogous to
Corollary

5.11
does not hold for even powers.
To this end, since the induced matching treewidth (resp. tree-independence number) of chordal graphs is at most one, it suffices to show
that the
k
-th powers of chordal graphs may have arbitrarily large induced matching treewidth (resp. tree-independence number). The idea of the construction below comes from Eto et al.
[
35
]
.

Lemma 5.16
.

Let
r
be an even positive integer. For any graph
H
, there exists a chordal graph
G
such that
G^{r}
contains
H
as an induced subgraph.

Proof.

Let
\widehat{H}
be the graph obtained from
H
by subdividing each edge exactly once and adding edges so that the newly introduced vertices form a clique. For convenience, we denote the vertices of
\widehat{H}

not
resulting from edge subdivisions by
V(H)
. Note that the vertices of
V(\widehat{H})\setminus V(H)
correspond to the edges of
H
.
Then
V(H)
is an independent set in
\widehat{H}
and
V(\widehat{H})\setminus V(H)
is a clique in
\widehat{H}
.
Hence
\widehat{H}
is a split graph and thus a chordal graph.
We obtain the graph
G
from
\widehat{H}
by appending to each vertex
v\in V(H)
a path
P^{v}
of length
(r-2)/2
(which is an integer, since
r
is even) such that one endpoint of
P^{v}
is
v
and all the other vertices of
P^{v}
are new.
In particular, for any two different vertices
v,w\in V(H)
, the corresponding paths
P^{v}
and
P^{w}
are vertex-disjoint.
By construction, the graph
G
is chordal.
For each vertex
v\in V(H)
, let us denote the two endpoints of
P^{v}
by
v
and
v^{\prime}
(with
v^{\prime}=v
if and only if
r=2
), and let
X=\{v^{\prime}\colon v\in V(H)\}
.
For arbitrary two distinct vertices
u,v\in V(H)
, it holds that
u^{\prime}v^{\prime}\in E(G^{r})
if and only if
\textsf{dist}_{G}(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})\leqslant r
, which happens if and only if
u
and
v
are adjacent in
H
.
Thus, the subgraph of
G^{r}
induced by
X
is isomorphic to
H
.
This shows that for every graph
H
there exists a chordal graph
G
such that
G^{r}
contains an induced subgraph isomorphic to
H
, as claimed.
∎

Since there exist graphs with arbitrarily large induced matching treewidth and tree-independence number, we obtain the following corollaries.

Corollary 5.17
.

Let
r
be an even positive integer.
There is no function
f
satisfying
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G^{r})\leqslant f(\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G))
for all graphs
G
.

Corollary 5.18
.

Let
r
be an even positive integer.
There is no function
f
satisfying
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G^{r})\leqslant f(\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G))
for all graphs
G
.

6
Max Weight Induced Subgraph for graphs with bounded tree-independence number

We start this section by recalling some basic notions and properties related to
\mathsf{CMSO}_{2}
.

6.1
\mathsf{CMSO}_{2}
: basic notions and properties

\mathsf{CMSO}_{2}
logic on graphs.

We assume that graphs are encoded as relational structures: each vertex and each edge is represented by a single variable (distinguishable by a unary predicate), and there is a single binary relation
\mathsf{inc}
(
incident
) binding each edge with its endvertices.

\mathsf{MSO}_{2}
(
Monadic Second Order
) logic is a logic on graphs that allows us to use vertex variables, edge variables, vertex set variables, and edge set variables,
and, furthermore, to quantify over them.
An example of an
\mathsf{MSO}_{2}
formula is the following expression checking if the chromatic number of a given graph is at most
r
(for constant
r
).
Here,
S_{i}
’s are vertex set variables,
e
is an edge variable, and
x,y
are vertex variables.

\displaystyle\begin{split}\exists_{S_{1},S_{2},\ldots,S_{r}}\;(\forall_{x}\;x\in S_{1}\cup S_{2}\ldots\cup S_{r})\;\land\forall_{e}\forall_{x,y}\;(\mathsf{inc}(e,x)\land\mathsf{inc}(e,y)\land x\neq y)\to\bigwedge_{i=1}^{r}(x\notin S_{i}\lor y\notin S_{i}).\end{split}

For a positive integer
p
, by
\mathsf{C}_{\leqslant p}\mathsf{MSO}_{2}
we mean the extension of
\mathsf{MSO}_{2}
that allows us to use atomic formulae of the form
|S|\equiv a\bmod b
, where
S
is a (vertex of edge) set variable, and
a,b\leqslant p
are integers.
We define
\mathsf{CMSO}_{2}
(
Counting Monadic Second Order
logic) to be
\bigcup_{p>0}
\mathsf{C}_{\leqslant p}\mathsf{MSO}_{2}
.
The
quantifier rank
of a formula is the maximum number of nested quantifiers in the formula (counting both first-order and second-order quantifiers).

Boundaried graphs and
\mathsf{CMSO}_{2}
types.

Let
\ell>0
be an integer.
An
\ell
-boundaried graph
is a pair
(G,\iota)
, where
G
is a graph and
\iota
is a partial injective function from
V(G)
to
[\ell]
. The domain of
\iota
, denoted by
\mathrm{dom}(\iota)
, is called the
boundary
of
(G,\iota)
. For
v\in\mathrm{dom}(\iota)
, the value of
\iota(v)
is called the
label
of
v
.

Let us define two natural operations on boundaried graphs.
For an
\ell
-boundaried graph
(G,\iota)
and
l\in[\ell]
,
the result of
forgetting the label
l
is the
\ell
-boundaried graph
(G,\iota_{\neg l})
,
here
\iota_{\neg l}\coloneqq\iota|_{\mathrm{dom}(\iota)\setminus\iota^{-1}(l)}
.
In other words, if the boundary of
(G,\iota)
contains vertex
v
with label
l
,
we remove the label from
v
, while keeping
v
in the graph. Note that if
l
is not in the image of
\iota
, then this operation does not do anything.

For two
\ell
-boundaried graphs
(G_{1},\iota_{1})
and
(G_{2},\iota_{2})
,
the result of
gluing
them is the
\ell
-boundaried graph
(G_{1},\iota_{1})\oplus_{[\ell]}(G_{2},\iota_{2})
obtained from the disjoint union of
(G_{1},\iota_{1})
and
(G_{2},\iota_{2})
by identifying vertices with the same label.
Note that it might happen that some label
l
is used in, e.g.,
\iota_{1}
but not in
\iota_{2}
. Then the vertex with label
l
in
(G_{1},\iota_{1})\oplus_{[\ell]}(G_{2},\iota_{2})
is the copy of the vertex with label
l
from
(G_{1},\iota_{1})
.

It is straightforward to observe that a graph has treewidth less than
\ell
if and only if it can be constructed from
\ell
-boundaried graphs with at most two vertices by a sequence of forgetting and gluing operations (see, e.g.,
[
36
]
).

By
\mathsf{CMSO}_{2}
in
\ell
-boundaried graphs we mean
\mathsf{CMSO}_{2}
extended with
\ell
unary predicates: for each
l\in[\ell]
we have a predicate that selects a vertex with label
l
(if such a vertex exists).
Let
(G,\iota)
be an
\ell
-boundaried graph and
p
and
q
be positive integers.
The
(p,q)
-type
of
(G,\iota)
is the set of all
\mathsf{C}_{\leqslant p}\mathsf{MSO}_{2}
formulae
\phi
of quantifier rank at most
q
such that
(G,\iota)\models\phi
.
As such, the
(p,q)
-type of an
\ell
-boundaried graph is an infinite set.
However, the formulae in
\mathsf{C}_{\leqslant p}\mathsf{MSO}_{2}
can be normalized so that every formula
can be effectively transformed into an equivalent normalized formula of the same quantifier rank.
Furthermore, for every quantifier rank there are only finitely many pairwise non-equivalent normalized formulae.
This result is folklore (see, e.g.,
[
39
, Lemma 6.1]
) and holds for general relational structures.
In the setting of
\ell
-boundaried graphs, the result is given by the following proposition.
The exact formulation we use here comes from Gartland et al.
[
38
, Proposition 8]
.

Proposition 6.1
.

For every triple of integers
\ell,p,q
, there exists a finite set
\mathsf{Types}^{\ell,p,q}
and a function that assigns to every
\ell
-boundaried graph
(G,\iota)
a
type

\mathsf{type}^{\ell,p,q}(G,\iota)\in\mathsf{Types}^{\ell,p,q}
such that the following holds:

1.

The types of isomorphic graphs are the same.

2.

For every
\mathsf{C}_{\leqslant p}\mathsf{MSO}_{2}
formula
\phi
on
\ell
-boundaried graphs, whether
(G,\iota)
satisfies
\phi
depends only on the type
\mathsf{type}^{\ell,p,q}(G,\iota)
, where
q
is the quantifier rank of
\phi
.
More precisely,
there exists a subset
\mathsf{Types}^{\ell,p,q}[\phi]\subseteq\mathsf{Types}^{\ell,p,q}

such that for every
\ell
-boundaried graph
(G,\iota)
we have

(G,\iota)\models\phi\qquad\textrm{if and only if}\qquad\mathsf{type}^{\ell,p,q}(G,\iota)\in\mathsf{Types}^{\ell,p,q}[\phi].

3.

The types of ingredients determine the type of the result of the gluing operation.
More precisely, for every two types
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in\mathsf{Types}^{\ell,p,q}

there exists a type
\tau_{1}\oplus_{\ell,p,q}\tau_{2}
such that
for every two
\ell
-boundaried graphs
(G_{1},\iota_{1})
,
(G_{2},\iota_{2})
,
if
\mathsf{type}^{\ell,p,q}(G_{i},\iota_{i})=\tau_{i}
for
i=1,2
, then

\mathsf{type}^{\ell,p,q}((G_{1},\iota_{1})\oplus_{[\ell]}(G_{2},\iota_{2}))=\tau_{1}\oplus_{\ell,p,q}\tau_{2}.

Also, the operation
\oplus_{\ell,p,q}
is associative and commutative.

4.

The type of the ingredient determines the type of the result of the forget label operation.
More precisely,
for every type
\tau\in\mathsf{Types}^{\ell,p,q}
and
l\in[\ell]

there exists a type
\tau_{\neg l}
such that
for every
\ell
-boundaried graph
(G,\iota)
,
if
\mathsf{type}^{\ell,p,q}(G,\iota)=\tau

and
(G,\iota_{\neg l})
is the result of forgetting
l
in
(G,\iota)
, then

\mathsf{type}^{\ell,p,q}(G,\iota_{\neg l})=\tau_{\neg l}.

Intuitively, for any
\ell
-boundaried graph
(G,\iota)
and every
\mathsf{C}_{\leqslant p}\mathsf{MSO}_{2}
formula
\phi
with quantified rank at most
q
, whether
(G,\iota)\models\phi
is fully determined by
\mathsf{type}^{\ell,p,q}(G,\iota)
.
Furthermore, if
\phi
and
\ell
are fixed, then the sets
\mathsf{Types}^{\ell,p,q}
and
\mathsf{Types}^{\ell,p,q}[\phi]
are of constant size.
Finally, it is known that there is an algorithm that, given
\ell
and
\phi
, computes
\mathsf{Types}^{\ell,p,q}
and
\mathsf{Types}^{\ell,p,q}[\phi]

[
22
]
.

6.2
\mathsf{CMSO}_{2}
-definable subgraphs of graphs with small tree-independence number

Now we are ready to prove our main result concerning sparse induced subgraphs satisfying some
\mathsf{CMSO}_{2}
-definable property.
Here, “sparse” means having bounded clique number.

For positive integers
a
and
b
, we denote by
\textsf{Ramsey}(a,b)
the smallest positive integer
n
such that every graph with at least
n
vertices contains either a clique of size
a
or an independent set of size
b
.
As proved by Ramsey
[
63
]
, these numbers are well-defined.

Theorem 6.2
.

For every
k
,
r
, and a
\mathsf{CMSO}_{2}
formula
\psi
there exists a positive integer
f(k,r,\psi)
such that the following holds.
Let
G
be a graph given along with a tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}=(T,\{X_{t}\}_{t\in V(T)})
of independence number at most
k
,
and let
\mathfrak{w}\colon V(G)\to\mathbb{Q}_{+}
be a weight function.
Then in time
f(k,r,\psi)\cdot|V(G)|^{\mathcal{O}(\textsf{Ramsey}(k+1,r+1))}\cdot|V(T)|
we can find a set
F\subseteq V(G)
, such that

1.

G[F]\models\psi
,

2.

\omega(G[F])\leqslant r
,

3.

F
is of maximum weight subject to the conditions above,

or conclude that no such set exists.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{T}=(T,\{X_{t}\}_{t\in V(T)})
be a tree decomposition of
G
with independence number at most
k
.
Recall that without loss of generality we can assume that
\mathcal{T}
is nice, since otherwise we can transform the given tree decomposition into a nice one in time
\mathcal{O}(|V(G)|^{2}|V(T)|)
.
Let
F
be a fixed (unknown) optimal solution. Note that by removing all vertices from
V(G)\setminus F
from each bag of
\mathcal{T}
, we obtain a tree decomposition of
G[F]
with independence number at most
k
.
As
\omega(G[F])\leqslant r
, we conclude that every bag of
\mathcal{T}
contains fewer than
\textsf{Ramsey}(k+1,r+1)
vertices of
F
.
In particular, the treewidth of
G[F]
is at most
\ell\coloneqq\textsf{Ramsey}(k+1,r+1)
.
This is the only way in which we use that
\omega(G[F])\leqslant r
.
Thus, in order to make sure that the obtained solution indeed satisfies the bound on the clique number,
instead of
\psi
we will use the
\mathsf{CMSO}_{2}
formula
\phi\coloneqq\psi\land\phi_{\omega\leqslant r}
, where
\phi_{\omega\leqslant r}
is the formula that bounds the clique number by
r
:

\forall_{v_{1},\ldots,v_{r+1}}\lnot\left(\bigwedge_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant r+1}\exists_{e}(\mathsf{inc}(e,v_{i})\land\mathsf{inc}(e,v_{j}))\right).

Since the treewidth of the graph
G[F]
is at most
\ell
, this graph can be constructed by a sequence of forgetting and gluing operations on
\ell
-boundaried graphs.

Consider the minimum integer
p
such that
\phi
belongs to
\mathsf{C}_{\leqslant p}\mathsf{MSO}_{2}
, and let
q
be the quantifier rank of
\phi
.
Let
\mathsf{Types}^{\ell,p,q}
and
\mathsf{Types}^{\ell,p,q}[\phi]
be as in
Proposition

6.1
.
Recall that these sets are of constant size (since
k,r
, and
\phi
are fixed); we assume that they are hard-coded in our algorithm.
Similarly, we assume that the forgetting and gluing operations on types, i.e., functions

(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\mapsto\tau_{1}\oplus_{\ell,p,q}\tau_{2}\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad(\tau,l)\mapsto\tau_{\neg l},

are hard-coded. Finally, our algorithm has the list of types of all
\ell
-boundaried graphs with at most
\ell
vertices.
As
\ell,p,q
are fixed throughout the algorithm, we will simplify the notation by dropping subscripts and superscripts, i.e., we will simply write
\mathsf{Types},\mathsf{Types}[\phi]
, and
\oplus
.

Our algorithm is a standard bottom-up dynamic programming on the nice tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}
of
G
.
We will describe how to compute the weight of an optimal solution; adapting our approach to return the solution itself is a standard task.

We introduce the dynamic programming table
\mathsf{Tab}_{\cdot}[\cdot]
indexed by nodes
t
of
T
and
states
.
Each state is a triple
(S,\iota,\tau)
, where
S\subseteq X_{t}
has at most
\ell
vertices,

\iota
is an injective function from
S
to
[\ell]
, and
\tau\in\mathsf{Types}
.
The value of
\mathsf{Tab}_{t}[S,\iota,\tau]
will encode the maximum weight of a subset
F_{t}
of
V(G_{t})
such that

1.

F_{t}\cap X_{t}=S
,

2.

\mathsf{type}((G[F_{t}],\iota))=\tau
.

Intuitively, we think of
S
as the intersection of a fixed optimal solution with
X_{t}
and we look for a maximum-weight induced
\ell
-boundaried subgraph of
G_{t}
with boundary
S
and type
\tau
.
If there is no subset
F_{t}
satisfying the conditions above, we will have
\mathsf{Tab}_{t}[S,\iota,\tau]=-\infty
.
Clearly, having computed all entries of
\mathsf{Tab}_{\cdot}[\cdot]
, the weight of the sought-for optimal solution is

\max_{\tau}\mathsf{Tab}_{\mathsf{root}(T)}[\emptyset,\emptyset,\tau],\text{ over all }\tau\in\mathsf{Types}[\phi].

Let us now describe how we compute the entries
\mathsf{Tab}_{t}[\cdot]
for a node
t
of
T
.
The exact way depends on the type of
x
.

Case:
t
is a leaf node.

Note that in this case
G_{t}
has no vertices, and thus the values of
\mathsf{Tab}_{t}[\cdot]
can be extracted from the information that is hard-coded in the algorithm.

Case:
t
is an introduce node.

Let
t^{\prime}
be the child of
t
in
T
, and let
v
be the unique vertex in
X_{t}\setminus X_{t^{\prime}}
.
Clearly, if
v\notin S
, then
\mathsf{Tab}_{t}[S,\iota,\tau]=\mathsf{Tab}_{t^{\prime}}[S,\iota,\tau]
.

So let
v\in S
, let
S^{\prime}=S\setminus\{v\}
, and let
\iota^{\prime}=\iota|_{S^{\prime}}
.
Let
G_{S}
be the subgraph of
X_{t}
induced by
S
. As
|S|\leqslant\ell
, the value
\tau_{S}\coloneqq\mathsf{type}((G_{S},\iota))
is hard-coded in our algorithm.
Now we set

\mathsf{Tab}_{t}[S,\iota,\tau]=\max_{\tau^{\prime}}\mathsf{Tab}_{t^{\prime}}[S^{\prime},\iota^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}],

where the maximum is taken over all
\tau^{\prime}
such that
\tau_{S}\oplus\tau^{\prime}=\tau
.

Case:
t
is a forget node.

Let
t^{\prime}
be the child of
t
in
\mathcal{T}
, and let
v
be the unique vertex in
X_{t^{\prime}}\setminus X_{t}
.
Intuitively, partial solutions at
t
correspond to partial solutions at
t^{\prime}
with the label of
v
(if any) forgotten.
Thus

\mathsf{Tab}_{t}[S,\iota,\tau]=\max\left(\mathsf{Tab}_{t^{\prime}}[S,\iota,\tau],\max_{\iota^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}}\mathsf{Tab}_{t^{\prime}}[S\cup\{v\},\iota^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}]\right),

where the second term only appears if
|S|<\ell
.
The maximum in the second term is computed over all pairs
\iota^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}
, such that
\iota^{\prime}|_{S}=\iota
and
\tau^{\prime}_{\neg\iota^{\prime}(v)}=\tau
.

Case:
t
is a join node.

Let
t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime}
be the children of
t
.
Thus

\mathsf{Tab}_{t}[S,\iota,\tau]=\max_{\tau^{\prime},\tau^{\prime\prime}}\mathsf{Tab}_{t^{\prime}}[S,\iota,\tau^{\prime}]+\mathsf{Tab}_{t^{\prime\prime}}[S,\iota,\tau^{\prime\prime}]-\mathfrak{w}(S),

where the maximum is taken over all
\tau^{\prime},\tau^{\prime\prime}
such that
\tau^{\prime}\oplus\tau^{\prime\prime}=\tau
. Note that we subtract the weight of
S
,
as it is counted both for
t^{\prime}
and for
t^{\prime\prime}
.

The correctness of the procedure above follows directly from the properties of nice tree decompositions and
Proposition

6.1
.

For each node
t
of
T
, the number of states is at most
|X_{t}|^{\ell}\cdot\ell!\cdot|\mathsf{Types}|
.
Each entry of
\mathsf{Tab}_{\cdot}[\cdot]
can be computed in time proportional to the number of states for each node (here we use that
k,r,\phi
are fixed).
We conclude that the running time is
f(k,r,\psi)\cdot|V(G)|^{\mathcal{O}(\textsf{Ramsey}(k+1,r+1))}|V(T)|
for some function
f
depending on
k,r
, and
\psi
.
∎

We remark that one can easily obtain a variant of
Theorem

6.2
in which a tree decomposition with small independence number is not needed in the input.
Indeed, combining
Theorem

6.2
with
Theorem

3.2
yields
Theorem

6.3
(with
g(k,r)=\mathcal{O}(\textsf{Ramsey}(8k+1,r+1))
).

Theorem 6.3
.

For every
k
,
r
, and a
\mathsf{CMSO}_{2}
formula
\psi
there exist positive integers
f(k,r,\psi)
and
g(k,r)
such that the following holds.
Let
G
be a graph with
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G)\leqslant k
, and let
\mathfrak{w}\colon V(G)\to\mathbb{Q}_{+}
be a weight function.
Then in time
f(k,r,\psi)\cdot|V(G)|^{g(k,r)}
we can find a set
F\subseteq V(G)
, such that

1.

G[F]\models\psi
,

2.

\omega(G[F])\leqslant r
,

3.

F
is of maximum weight subject to the conditions above,

or conclude that no such set exists.

6.3
A generalization

Theorem

6.3
can be slightly generalized so that we can maximize the weight of some carefully chosen subset of the solution.
The formula
\psi
uses this set as a free variable.

Theorem 6.4
.

For every
k
,
r
, and a
\mathsf{CMSO}_{2}
formula
\psi
with one free vertex-set variable, there exist positive integers
f(k,r,\psi)
and
g(k,r)
such that the following holds.
Let
G
be a graph with
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G)\leqslant k
, and let
\mathfrak{w}\colon V(G)\to\mathbb{Q}_{+}
be a weight function.
Then in time
f(k,r,\psi)\cdot|V(G)|^{g(k,r)}
we can find sets
X\subseteq F\subseteq V(G)
, such that:

1.

(G[F],X)\models\psi
,

2.

\omega(G[F])\leqslant r
,

3.

X
is of maximum possible weight, subject to conditions above,

or conclude that no such set exists.

Proof sketch.

The proof follows by a simple trick used by Gartland et al.
[
38
, Theorem 38]
.
We just sketch the argument here and refer the reader to
[
38
]
for more details.

For a graph
G
and a subset
M\subseteq V(G)
, by
\breve{G}
we denote the
forked version of

(G,M)
, which is obtained as follows.
We start the construction with a copy of
G
.
For every vertex
v
of
G
we introduce to
\breve{G}^{M}
three vertices of degree 1, each adjacent only to
v
.
For every vertex
v
of
M
we introduce to
\breve{G}^{M}
a two-edge path, whose one endvertex is identified with
v
.
If
G
is equipped with weights
\mathfrak{w}
, we set the weights
\breve{\mathfrak{w}}
of all vertices of
\breve{G}^{M}
to 0, with the exception that the value of
\mathfrak{w}(v)
for
v\in M
is transferred to the other endvertex of the two-edge path attached to
v
.

Note that if
F^{\prime}
is a forked version of some
(F,M)
, then
F
and
M
can be uniquely decoded from
F^{\prime}
.
Indeed,
F
is the subgraph of
F^{\prime}
induced by vertices of degree at least four, and vertices of
M
are those vertices of
F
that are adjacent (in
F^{\prime}
) to a vertex of degree 2.
Furthermore,
\omega(F^{\prime})\leqslant\max(\omega(F),2)
.

We apply the algorithm of
Theorem

3.2
to compute in time
|V(G)|^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
a tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}=(T,\{X_{t}\}_{t\in V(T)})
of
G
with independence number at most
8k
.
Moreover, observe that, given the tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}
, we can easily obtain a tree decomposition
\breve{\mathcal{T}}=(\breve{T},\{\breve{X}_{t}\}_{t\in V(\breve{T})})
of
\breve{G}^{V(G)}
with
\alpha(\breve{\mathcal{T}})\leqslant\alpha(\mathcal{T})
.
The idea is to call the algorithm from
Theorem

6.2
on
\breve{G}^{V(G)}
and a formula
\breve{\psi}
that for a graph
F^{\prime}
ensures that

•

if
F^{\prime}
is a forked version of some
(F,M)
, then
F^{\prime}\models\breve{\psi}
if and only if
(F,M)\models\psi
,

•

otherwise
F^{\prime}\not\models\breve{\psi}
.

Such a formula can be easily constructed in
\mathsf{CMSO}_{2}

[
38
]
.
We observe that the solution found by the algorithm satisfies the conditions stated in the theorem; again, see
[
38
, Theorem 38]
for details.
∎

6.4
Algorithmic applications and limitations

Using the framework of
Theorem

6.3
we can solve several well-studied problems for graphs of bounded tree-independence number:

•

Max Weight Independent Set
,

•

Max Weight Induced Forest
, which is equivalent by complementation to
Min Weight Feedback Vertex Set
,

•

Max Weight Induced Bipartite Subgraph
, which is equivalent by complementation to
Min Weight Odd Cycle Transversal
,

•

Max Weight Induced Odd Cactus
, which is equivalent by complementation to
Min Weight Even Cycle Transversal
(see e.g.
[
12
]
),

•

Max Weight Induced Planar Subgraph
, which is equivalent to
Planarization
(see e.g.
[
61
]
),

•

Defective Coloring
, where we ask for a vertex coloring using
\chi_{\textrm{d}}
colors in which each color class induced a subgraph of maximum degree bounded by
\Delta^{*}
, where both
\chi_{\textrm{d}}
and
\Delta^{*}
are fixed (see, e.g.,
[
10
]
), and

•

Max
k
-Dependent Set
, that is, maximum set of vertices inducing a subgraph of maximum degree at most
k
(see, e.g.,
[
42
]
), which is equivalent by complementation to
Bounded-Degree Vertex Deletion
.

In particular, these results generalize Theorems 23 and 25 from
[
10
]
, giving polynomial-time algorithms for
Defective Coloring
for fixed
\chi_{\textrm{d}}
and
\Delta^{*}
in graphs of bounded treewidth and chordal graphs, respectively.
They also solve an open problem from
[
42
]
regarding the complexity of
Max
k
-Dependent Set
on interval graphs (see
[
42
, Table 2]
).

Using the extension from
Theorem

6.4
we can solve some other problems, for example:

•

find the maximum number of pairwise independent induced cycles.

Note that the difference is that we maximize the
number
of cycles, and not their
total size
(or weight), as we could do using
Theorem

6.3
.
On other other hand, the number of induced cycles in a graph might be very large, so this problem cannot be solved using
Theorem

5.6
(or
Theorem

5.15
).
However, we can solve our problem by calling
Theorem

6.4
with the following parameters:

•

r=3
,

•

\psi
is the formula saying that
G[F]
is 2-regular (i.e., each component of
G[F]
is a cycle) and each vertex from
X
must be in a distinct connected component of
G[F]
, and

•

the weight of each vertex is 1.

In conclusion, let us point out that in
Theorems

6.3
,
6.2
and
6.4
we need to assume that the clique number of the solution is bounded by a constant.
Indeed, the property of being a clique can be easily expressed by a
\mathsf{CMSO}_{2}
formula as follows (here
x,y
are vertex variables and
e
is an edge variable):

\phi_{\textrm{clique}}\coloneqq\forall_{x,y}\left(x\neq y\to\exists_{e}\left(\textsf{inc}(e,x)\land\textsf{inc}(e,y)\right)\right).

However,
Max Clique
is
NP
-hard on graphs with independence number 2, and thus of tree-independence number at most 2. Indeed, this follows from the fact that
Max Independent Set
is
NP
-hard on triangle-free graphs
[
62
]
, by complementing the input graph.

7
Complexity of computing induced matching treewidth and bounds

In this section, we collect some basic complexity and algorithmic observations about induced matching treewidth, establish its close relation to treewidth for graphs with bounded degree, and give constructions of two families of graphs with unbounded degree and unbounded induced matching treewidth.

7.1
Remarks on complexity of computing induced matching treewidth

Proposition

3.1
together with known hardness results on the tree-independence number from
[
32
,
29
]
and on the independence number from
[
73
]
imply the following hardness results for induced matching treewidth.

Theorem 7.1
.

The following lower bounds hold.

1.

For every constant
k\geqslant 4
, it is
NP
-complete to decide whether
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G)\leqslant k
for a graph
G
.

2.

For every
\varepsilon>0
, there is no polynomial-time algorithm for approximating induced matching treewidth of an
n
-vertex graph to within a factor of
n^{1-\varepsilon}
unless
\textsf{P}=\textsf{NP}
.

3.

There is no constant-factor
FPT
-approximation algorithm for induced matching treewidth, unless
\textsf{FPT}=\textsf{W[1]}
.

4.

For any computable functions
f,g
, there is no
g(k)
-approximation algorithm for computing induced matching treewidth in time
f(k)\cdot n^{o(k)}
, unless the Gap-ETH fails.
5
5

5

Gap-ETH states that for some constant
\epsilon>0
, distinguishing between a satisfiable 3-
SAT
formula and one that is not even
(1-\epsilon)
-satisfiable requires exponential time (see
[
52
,
33
]
).

Proof.

Fix an integer
k\geqslant 4
.
Clearly, the problem of testing if
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G)\leqslant k
for a given graph
G
belongs to
NP
.
To establish
NP
-hardness, we make a reduction from the problem of testing if a given graph
G
satisfies
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G)\leqslant k
.
As shown by Dallard et al.
[
29
]
, this problem is
NP
-complete.
By
Proposition

3.1
,
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G)=\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G\circ K_{1})
and hence
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G)\leqslant k
if and only if
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G\circ K_{1})\leqslant k
.
Since computing the corona of
G
can be done in polynomial time, hardness follows.

To obtain the inapproximability result, let us recall the original reduction establishing
NP
-hardness of the problem of computing the tree-independence number due to Dallard et al.
[
32
]
.
The reduction was from the independent set problem and was based on the fact that for any graph
G
, if
G^{\prime}
is the graph obtained from two disjoint copies of
G
by adding all possible edges between them, then
\alpha(G)=\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G^{\prime})
.
Combining this result with
Proposition

3.1
, we obtain that
\alpha(G)=\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G^{\prime})=\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G^{\prime}\circ K_{1})
.
Given an
n
-vertex graph
G
, the graph
G^{\prime}\circ K_{1}
can be computed in polynomial time from
G
and has
4n
vertices.
Now, approximately computing the induced matching treewidth of the transformed graph would imply an equally good approximation to the independence number of
G
.

Thus statements 2., 3., 4. follow from analogous hardness results for approximating
Max Independent Set
, shown by, respectively,
Zuckerman
[
73
]
, Lin
[
50
]
, and Chalersmook et al.
[
26
]
.
∎

Let us remark that
Theorem

7.1
does not leave much space for improvements to
Theorem

3.3
.
First, it implies that we cannot
exactly
compute induce matching treewidth, even for small values (at least 4).
Second, we cannot hope for a nontrivial approximation in polynomial time.
Finally, the running time of the approximation algorithm cannot be improved to
f(k)\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
and even
f(k)\cdot n^{o(k)}
, under standard complexity assumptions.

Theorem

7.1
establishes
NP
-completeness of recognizing graphs with induced matching width at most
k
, for every integer
k\geqslant 4
.
The complexity of recognizing graphs with induced matching treewidth at most
k
is open for
k\in\{2,3\}
, but solvable in polynomial time for
k\leqslant 1
, as we discuss next.
Before doing that, let us show a nice property of induced matching treewidth that is also satisfied by tree-independence number (see
[
32
]
) and by
sim-width
, a graph parameter introduced in 2017 by Kang, Kwon, Strømme, and Telle (see
[
44
]
): the parameter is monotone under induced minors.
Given two graphs
G
and
H
, we say that
H
is an
induced minor
of
G
if a graph isomorphic to
H
can be obtained from
G
by a sequence of vertex deletions and edge contractions.

Proposition 7.2
.

Let
G
be a graph and let
H
be an induced minor of
G
.
Then
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(H)\leqslant\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G)
.

Proof.

We show how to modify any tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}=(T,\{X_{t}\}_{t\in V(T)})
of
G
into a tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
of
H
such that the induced matching treewidths of the two decompositions are related as follows:
\mu_{H}(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})\leqslant\mu_{G}(\mathcal{T})
.
This will imply the desired inequality
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(H)\leqslant\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G)
.

Clearly, it suffices to consider the case when
H
is obtained from
G
either by a single vertex deletion or by a single edge contraction.

Consider first the case when
H
is the graph obtained from
G
by deleting some vertex, say
v\in V(G)
.
Then deleting
v
from all the bags of
\mathcal{T}
that contain
v
results in a tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
of
H
.
Furthermore, since every induced matching in
H
is also an induced matching in
G
, it holds that
\mu_{G}(\mathcal{T})\geqslant\mu_{H}(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})
, as claimed.

Next, consider the case when
H
is the graph obtained from
G
by contracting an edge
uv\in E(G)
into a single vertex
z
.
In this case, it is not difficult to see that replacing each bag
X_{t}
of
\mathcal{T}
that contains either
u
or
v
(or both) with the bag
X_{t}^{\prime}\coloneqq(X_{t}\setminus\{u,v\})\cup\{z\}
results in a tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
of
H
.
We show that
\mu_{G}(\mathcal{T})\geqslant\mu_{H}(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})
by verifying that for every node
t\in V(T)
and every induced matching
M^{\prime}
in
H
that touches the bag
X_{t}^{\prime}
, there exists an induced matching
M
in
G
that touches the bag
X_{t}
and such that
|M|=|M^{\prime}|
.
This will indeed suffice, since taking
M^{\prime}
to be a maximum induced matching in
H^{\prime}
that touches some bag, say
X_{t}^{\prime}
, of
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
, then the corresponding induced matching
M
in
G
that touches the bag
X_{t}
will imply that

\mu_{G}(\mathcal{T})\geqslant|M|=|M^{\prime}|=\mu_{H}(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})
.

So, let
t\in V(T)
be a node of
T
and let
M^{\prime}
be an induced matching in
H
that touches the bag
X_{t}^{\prime}
.
We consider two cases depending on whether the vertex
z
is an endpoint of an edge in
M^{\prime}
or not.

Suppose first that the vertex
z
is not an endpoint of any edge in
M^{\prime}
.
Since the graph
H-z
is an induced subgraph of
G
, the matching
M^{\prime}
is also an induced matching in
G
.
Furthermore, since the matching
M^{\prime}
touches the bag
X_{t}^{\prime}
in vertices different from
z
, the fact that
X_{t}^{\prime}\setminus\{z\}\subseteq X_{t}
implies that
M^{\prime}
also touches the bag
X_{t}
.
Hence we can take
M=M^{\prime}
in this case.

Suppose next that the vertex
z
is an endpoint of an edge in
M^{\prime}
.
In this case there is a unique edge
e\in M^{\prime}
such that
z
is an endpoint of
e
.
We consider two further subcases depending on whether
z
belongs to the bag
X_{t}^{\prime}
or not.
Suppose first that
z\in X_{t}^{\prime}
.
In this case,
X_{t}^{\prime}=(X_{t}\setminus\{u,v\})\cup\{z\}
and the set
M\coloneqq(M^{\prime}\setminus\{e\})\cup\{uv\}
is an induced matching in
G
such that
|M|=|M^{\prime}|
.
Furthermore, since the matching
M\setminus\{uv\}=M^{\prime}\setminus\{e\}
touches the set
X_{t}\setminus\{u,v\}=X_{t}^{\prime}\setminus\{z\}
and the edge
uv
touches
X_{t}
(since
z\in X_{t}^{\prime}
), we infer that
M
touches
X_{t}
.

Finally, suppose that
z\not\in X_{t}^{\prime}
.
Then
X_{t}^{\prime}=X_{t}
.
Let
y
be the endpoint of
e
other than
z
.
Since the edge
e
touches the bag
X_{t}
and
z\not\in X_{t}^{\prime}
, we have
y\in X_{t}
.
Moreover,
y
is a vertex of
G
such that
uy\in E(G)
or
vy\in E(G)
.
We may assume without loss of generality that
uy\in E(G)
.
Then, the set
M\coloneqq(M^{\prime}\setminus\{e\})\cup\{uy\}
is an induced matching in
G
such that
|M|=|M^{\prime}|
.
Furthermore, since the matching
M\setminus\{uy\}=M^{\prime}\setminus\{e\}
touches the set
X_{t}=X_{t}^{\prime}
and the edge
uy
touches
X_{t}
, we infer that
M
touches
X_{t}
.
This completes the proof.
∎

Proposition

7.2
implies the following.
For an integer
k\geqslant 0
, let
\mathcal{F}_{k}
denote the family of all
forbidden induced minors
for the class of graphs with induced matching treewidth at most
k
, that is,
\mathcal{F}_{k}
consists precisely of the graphs
G
such that
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G)>k
but every proper induced minor
H
of
G
satisfies
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(H)\leqslant k
.
Then, by

Proposition

7.2
, a graph
G
has induced matching treewidth at most
k
if and only if
G
does not have any graph from
\mathcal{F}_{k}
as an induced minor.

It is not difficult to see that the only graphs with induced matching treewidth equal to
0
are exactly the edgeless graphs; hence
\mathcal{F}_{0}=\{K_{2}\}
.
To characterize graphs with induced matching treewidth at most
1
, we will need the following result due to Scheidweiler and Wiederrecht
[
67
, Theorem 3.6]
.
We denote by
C_{k}
the
k
-vertex cycle graph.

Theorem 7.3
.

There exists a finite set
\mathcal{F}
of graphs such that for every graph
G
, the graph
L^{2}(G)
is chordal if and only if
G
does not contain any graph from the set
\mathcal{F}\cup\{C_{k}:k\geqslant 6\}
as an induced subgraph.

We now have everything ready to prove the following.

Proposition 7.4
.

The class of graphs with induced matching treewidth at most
1
is characterized by finitely many forbidden induced minors and admits a polynomial-time recognition algorithm.

Proof.

By
Proposition

3.1
, for every graph
G
, it holds that
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G)=\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(L^{2}(G))
.
Recall also that a graph
G
is chordal if and only if
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G)\leqslant 1
.
It follows that a graph
G
has induced matching treewidth at most
1
if and only if the square of its line graph is chordal.
By
Theorem

7.3
, the class of graphs with this property is characterized by a family of forbidden induced subgraphs consisting of all cycles of length at least
6
and a family
\mathcal{F}
of finitely many other graphs.
Since each forbidden induced minor is also a forbidden induced subgraph, the family of forbidden induced minors is a subfamily of
\mathcal{F}\cup\{C_{k}:k\geqslant 6\}
.
Using also the fact that the
6
-cycle is an induced minor of any longer cycle, we infer that the set
\mathcal{F}_{1}
of forbidden induced minors for the class of graphs with induced matching treewidth at most
1
consists of the cycle
C_{6}
and a subfamily of
\mathcal{F}
; in particular, it is finite.

Given a graph
G
, testing if the square of its line graph is chordal can be done in polynomial time (using, e.g.,
[
66
]
).
Therefore, graphs with induced matching treewidth at most
1
can be recognized in polynomial time.
∎

7.2
Induced matching treewidth in graphs with bounded degree

Recall that the tree-independence number of a graph is an
upper bound on its induced matching treewidth, hence, any graph class with unbounded induced matching treewidth is necessarily of unbounded tree-independence number.
On the other hand, large tree-independence number does not necessarily imply large induced matching treewidth, as shown, for example, by the family of complete bipartite graphs.
The balanced complete bipartite graphs
K_{n,n}
satisfy
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(K_{n,n})=n
(see
[
32
]
) and
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(K_{n,n})=1
, as witnessed by the trivial tree decomposition having a unique bag equal to the entire vertex set.

Interestingly, in the absence of complete bipartite graphs as subgraphs, bounded induced matching treewidth implies bounded tree-independence number and even bounded treewidth.
This follows from a result of Brettell et al.
[
24
, Proposition 18]
who proved a related result for sim-width.
Brettell et al. showed that for any two integers
k,t\in\mathbb{N}
there exists an integer
r
such that each graph with sim-width at most
k
and no subgraph isomorphic to
K_{t,t}
has treewidth at most
r
.
It is known that bounded induced matching treewidth implies bounded sim-width.
This is explicitly shown in
[
14
, Theorem 24]
(see also
[
15
]
) but in fact the argument boils down to the proof of
[
44
, Proposition 3.1]
.
The formal statement of the result for induced matching treewidth is as follows.

Theorem 7.5
.

For any two integers
k,t\in\mathbb{N}
there exists an integer
r
such that each graph with induced matching treewidth at most
k
and no subgraph isomorphic to
K_{t,t}
has treewidth at most
r
.

Note that graphs with maximum degree less than
t
have no subgraph isomorphic to
K_{t,t}
.
Hence,
Theorem

7.5
implies that any class of graphs with bounded degree and unbounded treewidth has unbounded induced matching treewidth.
This includes, for example, the classes of (subdivided) square or hexagonal grids (also known as
walls
) and their line graphs.

An equivalent formulation is that in classes of graphs with bounded induced matching treewidth, bounded degree implies bounded treewidth.
This implication (that bounded degree implies bounded treewidth) was recently established by Abrishami, Chudnovsky, and Vušković
[
4
]
for the class of even-hole-free graphs, and by Korhonen
[
46
]
for any class of graphs excluding some fixed grid (or equivalently, some fixed planar graph) as an induced minor, as well as for any class of graphs excluding all sufficiently large walls and their line graphs as induced subgraphs.

The proof of
Theorem

7.5
via
[
24
]
is based on Ramsey-type arguments and the binding function is quite large (in particular, it is exponential in
t
).
We now give a simple argument showing that for graphs with bounded degree, the resulting bound on treewidth is linear in
k
and polynomial in the maximum degree.
We first show that for graphs with bounded degree, induced matching treewidth is linearly related to the tree-independence number.
As usual, for a graph
G
, we denote by
\Delta(G)
the maximum degree of a vertex in
G
.

Theorem 7.6
.

For every graph
G
with at least one edge, it holds that
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G)\leqslant 2\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G)\cdot\Delta(G)^{2}
.

Proof.

Let
G
be a graph with at least one edge.
If
G
has an isolated vertex, we can delete it without changing the value of any of the three parameters
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(G)
,
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G)
, and
\Delta(G)
, since they are all strictly positive.
We may therefore assume that
G
has no isolated vertices.
To shorten the notation we will denote
\Delta\coloneqq\Delta(G)
.
Let
\mathcal{T}=(T,\{X_{t}\}_{t\in V(T)})
be a tree decomposition of
G
witnessing
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G)
.
For all
t\in V(T)
, let

X^{\prime}_{t}=N[X_{t}]
and let
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}=(T,\{X^{\prime}_{t}\}_{t\in V(T)})
.
It is straightforward to verify that
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
is a tree decomposition of
G
.
We want to argue that the independence number of each bag of
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
is at most
2\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G)\cdot\Delta^{2}
.

Consider a bag
X^{\prime}_{t}
of
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
.
It consists of
X_{t}
and
Y=N(X_{t})
.
Let
I
be a largest independent set in
G[X^{\prime}_{t}]
and let
k
be its size.
We iteratively construct an induced matching
M
in
G
whose every edge intersects
X_{t}
and
|M|\geqslant k/(2\Delta^{2})
.
As
|M|\leqslant\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G)
, this will give
|I|=k\leqslant\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G)\cdot 2\Delta^{2}
.

At the beginning, the matching
M
is empty.
In each step of the procedure we add one edge to
M
and discard some set of vertices.
The procedure stops when all the vertices in
I
are discarded.
At each step of the procedure we want to preserve the following properties:

1.

Each
v\in I
that is not discarded has a non-discarded neighbor in
X^{\prime}_{t}
.

2.

Each
v\in I\cap Y
that is not discarded has a non-discarded neighbor in
X_{t}
.

Clearly the above properties are satisfied at the beginning, before any vertex is discarded, as the graph has no isolated vertices and every vertex from
I\cap Y
has a neighbor in
X_{t}
.

Now pick any vertex
v
in
I
that was not discarded yet.
By properties 1. and 2.
v
has a non-discarded neighbor
w
, and if
v\in Y
, then
w
can be chosen to be in
X_{t}
.
We then include the edge
vw
in the induced matching
M
.

Next, we discard the following vertices from
X^{\prime}_{t}
:

•

v
and
w
,

•

every non-discarded neighbor of
v
or
w
, and

•

every non-discarded vertex in
I
that is at distance 2 from
\{v,w\}
in
G[X^{\prime}_{t}]
.

Notice that since we removed
N[\{u,v\}]\cap X^{\prime}_{t}
, the edges chosen in every subsequent iteration will indeed form an induced matching.
In each iteration we discard at most
2\Delta^{2}
vertices from
I
, so,
provided that properties 1. and 2. are preserved, the number of iterations we perform to build an induced matching
M
of the desired size is at most
k/(2\Delta^{2})
.
Note also that every edge of
M
intersects
X_{t}
.

Finally, we prove that at each step of the procedure properties 1. and 2. hold.
To this end, it suffices to show that for each
u\in I
that is not discarded, its entire neighborhood in
X^{\prime}_{t}
is not discarded.
Suppose for a contradiction that there exists some vertex
u^{\prime}\in N(u)\cap X^{\prime}_{t}
that is discarded.
Clearly
u^{\prime}\notin I
, so
u^{\prime}
must be equal or adjacent to
v
or
w
.
However, this means that
u
is at distance at most 2 from
\{v,w\}
in
X^{\prime}_{t}
, so
u
got discarded, a contradiction.
∎

Corollary 7.7
.

For every graph
G
with at least one edge, it holds that

\mathrm{tw}(G)\leqslant 2\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G)\cdot\Delta(G)^{2}(\Delta(G)+1)\,.

Proof.

Let
G
be a graph with at least one edge, let
\Delta
be its maximum degree and let
k
be its induced matching treewidth.
By
Theorem

7.6
,
G
admits a tree decomposition in which each bag induces a subgraph with independence number at most
2k\Delta^{2}
.
Since the maximum degree of any such subgraph is at most
\Delta
, its chromatic number is at most
\Delta+1
.
Consequently, every bag has at most
2k\Delta^{2}(\Delta+1)
vertices.
∎

7.3
Two graph families with unbounded induced matching treewidth

We conclude this section by identifying two graph families with unbounded maximum degree and unbounded induced matching treewidth.
For the proofs, we need the following well-known property of tree decompositions (see, e.g.,
[
32
]
).

Lemma 7.8
.

For every graph
G
and every tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}
of
G
, there exists a vertex
v\in V
such that
N[v]
is contained in some bag of
\mathcal{T}
.

For a positive integer
n
, the
n
-dimensional hypercube graph
is the graph
Q_{n}
with vertex set
\{0,1\}^{n}
in which two vertices
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
and
y=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})
are adjacent if and only if there exists a unique
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
such that
x_{i}\neq y_{i}
.

Proposition 7.9
.

The
n
-dimensional hypercube graph
Q_{n}
has
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(Q_{n})\geqslant\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor
.

Proof.

Consider an arbitrary tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}=(T,\{X_{t}\}_{t\in V(T)})
of
Q_{n}
.
By
Lemma

7.8
, there exists a bag
X_{t}
that contains
N[v]
for some
v\in V(Q_{n})=\{0,1\}^{n}
.
By the symmetry of
Q_{n}
, we may assume without loss of generality that
v=(0,\ldots,0)
.

Let
v_{i}
denote the vertex whose only non-zero entry is at the
i
-th position and let
w_{i,j}
denote the vertex whose only non-zero entries are the
i
-th and
j
-th position.

An induced matching
M
touching
N[v]
(and thus touching the bag) of size
\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor
is as follows

M=\left\{v_{2i}w_{2i,2i-1}\large\mid i\in\left\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\right\rfloor\right\}.

This implies that
\mu(\mathcal{T})
is at least
\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor
.
Since
\mathcal{T}
was arbitrary, we infer that
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(Q_{n})\geqslant\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor
.
∎

Our final result gives a simple construction of a family of
P_{4}
-free graphs with unbounded induced matching number.
Recall that for a positive integer
n
, we denote by
nK_{2}
the disjoint union of
n
copies of
K_{2}
.

Proposition 7.10
.

Let
n
be a positive integer and let
G_{n}
denote the graph obtained from two disjoint copies of the graph
nK_{2}
by adding all possible edges between them.
Then
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(G_{n})\geqslant n
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{T}=(T,\{X_{t}\}_{t\in V(T)})
be an arbitrary tree decomposition of
G_{n}
.
By
Lemma

7.8
, there exists some bag
X_{t}
containing
N[v]
for some
v\in V(G_{n})
. By definition of
G_{n}
, the bag
X_{t}
contains all the vertices of one of the copies of
nK_{2}
, which form an induced matching of size
n
.
∎

8
Conclusion

The obvious direction for further research is to show
1.2
.
A somewhat easier, but still very interesting problem, would be to show the following result, which is a significant common generalization of both
Theorem

2.1
and
Corollary

2.4
.

Conjecture 8.1
.

Let
k,r\in\mathbb{N}
be fixed.
Let
G
be a graph with induced matching treewidth at most
k
, equipped with a weight function
\mathfrak{w}\colon V(G)\to\mathbb{Q}_{+}
.
In polynomial time we can find a maximum-weight induced subgraph of
G
with treewidth at most
r
.

We believe that the right way of approaching
8.1
is to prove an analogue of
Theorem

3.4
and
Lemma

4.1
:
for each node of a tree decomposition of bounded induced matching treewidth, there is only a polynomial number of states of the natural dynamic programming algorithm (developed for bounded-treewidth graphs
[
20
]
) that represent an inclusion-maximal induced subgraph of treewidth at most
r
. However, the space of states in these algorithms is quite complicated (much more complicated than when finding a largest induced forest) and it is far from obvious how to re-interpret it in the current setting.

Another interesting question is to try to find a “better parameter” than induced matching width.
If we think of a parameter useful for solving
MWIS
, it is natural to expect that for a simple graph such that
K_{n,n}\odot K_{1}
, i.e., a biclique with additional pending edge added to each vertex, such a parameter would be bounded. However, by
Proposition

3.1
, we have
{\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(K_{n,n}\odot K_{1})}=\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\alpha(K_{n,n})=n
.

Recently, Bergougnoux et al.
[
15
]
introduced
one-sided mim-width
(or
o-mim-width
), which can be seen as a unification of tree-independence number and
mim-width
, which is another well-established graph width parameter useful in solving
MWIS
and many other problems
[
13
]
.
Just like the definition of induced matching treewidth, the definitions of mim-width and one-sided mim-width are also based on the size of a maximum induced matching in certain subgraphs of the graph (however, instead of tree decompositions they are based on branch decompositions).
While induced matching treewidth lower-bounds the tree-independence number,
it turns out that it is incomparable with one-sided mim-width (and mim-width).
Bergougnoux et al.
[
15
]
proved a result analogous to
Theorem

2.1
for graphs given with a decomposition with bounded one-sided mim-width.

Another interesting parameter based on induced matchings and that in fact captures all these notions (o-mim-width, mim-width, tree-independence number, and induced matching treewidth) is sim-width.
As already mentioned, bounded induced matching treewidth implies bounded sim-width, but not vice versa; for example, the graphs from
Proposition

7.10
, which have unbounded induced matching treewidth, have bounded sim-width (see, e.g.,
[
44
, Proposition 3.6]
).
One of the main open problems related to sim-width, first asked in
[
44
]
, is whether
MWIS
is solvable in polynomial time for graphs with bounded sim-width.
If the answer is positive, then results from
[
55
,
24
]
imply that the same is true for
Max Weight Distance-
d
Packing
for all even
d
.
The drawback however is that a branch decomposition of small sim-width is needed in input, as it is another open problem to determine whether such decompositions can be obtained in polynomial time.

The relationships between all these parameters and some others are summarized in
Figure

2
.

Figure 2:
Relations between some graph width parameters.

Recall that classes of bounded tree-independence number are
(\mathrm{tw},\omega)
-bounded, i.e., treewidth is bounded by a function of the clique number
[
31
]
. By
Theorem

7.5
we know that this is also the case for classes of bounded induced matching treewidth that additionally exclude some fixed biclique as a subgraph.
Furthermore, excluding bicliques is necessary, as
\mathrm{tw}(K_{n,n})=n
and
\mathsf{tree}\textnormal{-}\mu(K_{n,n})=1
.
We believe that the following result, which is a common generalization of
Theorems

7.6
and
7.5
, could also hold.

Conjecture 8.2
.

For any two integers
k,t\in\mathbb{N}
there exists an integer
r
such that each graph with induced matching treewidth at most
k
and no induced subgraph isomorphic to
K_{t,t}
has tree-independence number at most
r
.

The notion of
(\mathrm{tw},\omega)
-boundedness can be seen as a variant of the extensively studied
\chi
-boundedness
, where we ask for which classes the chromatic number is upper-bounded by a function of the clique number
[
68
]
. We believe that graphs of bounded induced matching treewidth are
\chi
-bounded.

Conjecture 8.3
.

For any two integers
k,c\in\mathbb{N}
there exists an integer
r
such that each graph with induced matching treewidth at most
k
and clique number at most
c
has chromatic number at most
r
.

Finally, we believe that it would be interesting to explore for what natural classes of graphs induced matching treewidth is bounded. We remark that such results are known for the closely related tree-independence number
[
1
,
30
]
.
